2 
Introduction 
This Geotechnical Engineering Report (this Report) has been prepared by Aurecon on behalf of the 
Department Planning, Housing and Infrastructure (DPHI) to support the State-led rezoning of the Macquarie 
Coal Complex for Post Mining Land Use. The Macquarie Coal Complex Land Transformation Precinct (the 
Precinct) and Teralba Southgate form part of the NSW Government’s investigation into future land use 
pathways for mining affected land. 
This Report provides a desktop based geotechnical assessment to support the rezoning, planning and 
staged redevelopment of the Precinct and Teralba Southgate sub-precinct. It establishes an evidence base 
to identify, assess and manage geotechnical constraints, and to guide investigation, earthworks and 
foundation design requirements across in these areas. 
The Report is intended to inform: 
 State-led rezoning processes 
 Structure planning and sub‑precinct development strategies 
 Infrastructure planning and delivery 
 Future development applications and site-specific investigations. 
The Precinct and Teralba Southgate are characterised by a highly variable ground environment, shaped by: 
 Underground mining (West Wallsend Colliery) 
 Open cut mining and backfilled voids 
 Coal processing infrastructure and tailings deposition 
 Progressive rehabilitation and landform recontouring  
These legacy conditions result in a range of geotechnical challenges, including: 
 Mine subsidence risk 
 Variable and uncontrolled fill 
 Soft and compressible alluvial soils 
 Groundwater variability 
 Stability and settlement considerations associated with the Tailings Storage Facility (TSF). 
A strategic, risk-informed framework to manage constraints associated with the geotechnical conditions of 
the Precinct and Teralba Southgate should therefore be adopted.  
2.1 
Background 
Open cut and underground mining operations have all ceased and current activities are focused on 
rehabilitation, closure planning, and long-term environmental management.  
The Macquarie Coal Complex is a decommissioned mining operation previously operated by Glencore, 
which processed coal sourced from the nearby underground West Wallsend Colliery and the Westside open-
cut mining operations. Mining activities at West Wallsend Colliery, Westside open-cut mining operation and 
Teralba Southgate respectively ceased in 2016, 2012 and 2001. 
The Structure Plan has divided the Precinct and Teralba Southgate into three post mining land use land 
categories for conservation, development and recreation purposes, further divided into six sub-precinct areas 
as shown Figure 2-1 and listed in Table 2-1.
`

### Geotechnical Report Page 11
`
Project number 531168-000  File Geotechnical Engineering Report Rev B_Clean.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
6 
 
 
 
 
 
This Report has been prepared using publicly available information together with existing project data, 
including previous geotechnical investigations, borehole logs, laboratory testing records, mine rehabilitation 
documentation, and available topographic and subsidence information. 
Specifically, the Report aims to: 
 Provide a rezoning/master plan level geotechnical evidence base to verify developable land and inform 
land use compatibility, built form intensity and infrastructure feasibility 
 Translate Enquiry by Design (EbD) identified constraints, including soils, steep terrain constraints and 
mine subsidence district considerations, into Master Plan mapping, performance criteria and staging 
triggers 
 Provide a clear pathway for later-phase investigations (intrusive testing, stability modelling, settlement 
monitoring) without duplicating Development Application (DA) stage work. 
2.3 
Assumptions and limitations 
This Report has been prepared to inform a Master Plan for post mining land use at the rezoning stage.  The 
assessment is based on existing available information and does not constitute a DA level geotechnical 
design assessment.  
The following limitations apply to this assessment: 
 This assessment has been limited to publicly accessible data available to Aurecon and that provided by 
DPHI, and Glencore 
 No geotechnical or hydrogeological modelling has been undertaken for the purpose of this assessment 
 No detailed development analysis has been undertaken as part of geotechnical assessment 
 No geotechnical investigations were carried out for this assessment. 
The following items are specifically excluded from, or deferred beyond, the scope of this assessment: 
 Comprehensive intrusive site investigation campaigns across the full Precinct and Teralba Southgate 
 Detailed stability modelling, settlement predictions and foundation design 
 DA stage geotechnical design certification and construction-phase verification 
 Detailed data gaps identified for the geotechnical constraint assessment are further outlined in Section 
5.2 
 Mine subsidence is assumed to apply across the entire Precinct and Teralba Southgate, and is treated as 
a site-wide constraint rather than a differentiating factor between sub-precincts.
`

## 4. Contamination, TSF & Dams

### Contamination Report Page 3
`
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
 
 
 
Executive summary 
This Contamination Technical Assessment (CTA) has been prepared by Aurecon Australasia Pty Ltd 
(Aurecon) on behalf of the Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure (DPHI) to support the State-
led rezoning of the Macquarie Coal Complex (the Precinct) and Teralba Southgate, located approximately 20 
km southwest of Newcastle within the Lake Macquarie region, NSW. 
The purpose of this CTA is to reinterpret existing contamination data, originally collected to support mine 
closure and rehabilitation, within the context of proposed rezoning and future land use scenarios identified in 
the Structure and Master Plan and Teralba Southgate. The assessment is scoped at the rezoning stage to 
inform land use compatibility and identification of developable land. It does not constitute development 
application (DA) level investigation or detailed remediation design. 
Site Context 
The Precinct comprises four former mining operations: including West Wallsend Colliery, Macquarie Coal, 
Preparation Plant, Westside Mine (open-cut), and Teralba Colliery (Northgate). Teralba Southgate is located 
approximately 2.5 kilometres south of the Precinct and is occupied by former mine infrastructure including 
shafts. Mining activities ceased progressively between 2001 and 2016. Current activities are limited to 
environmental management and rehabilitation in accordance with the Rehabilitation Management Plan (RMP 
2022). The Precinct and Teralba Southgate are surrounded primarily by residential and mixed-use industrial 
areas.  
The rezoning aims to facilitate employment-led development supported by residential, conservation, and 
open space land uses. A figure showing the extent of Teralba Southgate and the current Structure Plan for 
the Precinct is provided in Figure E1. 
 
Figure E1 – Layout and extent of Teralba Southgate and the Structure Plan for the Precinct
`

### Contamination Report Page 5
`
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
 
 
 
 
Figure E2 – Location and extent of the closure domains at the Precinct and Teralba Southgate 
Data Gaps and Limitations 
Significant data gaps limit ability for full characterisation of contamination and land suitability, including: 
◼ Multiple domains with no intrusive investigation (lacking soil, groundwater, surface water and sediment 
data). 
◼ Incomplete ASS and groundwater characterisation. 
◼ Limited assessment beneath buildings and inaccessible areas/structures. 
◼ Incomplete delineation of buried asbestos materials. 
◼ Limited ground gas assessments. 
Land Use Suitability 
Based on available data and acknowledging limitations: 
◼ Commercial and industrial uses - Generally compatible across most of the Precinct and Teralba 
Southgate, subject to targeted investigation and management. 
◼ Sensitive uses (residential, conservation, recreational) - Require further investigation and risk assessment 
to confirm suitability. 
◼ Key constraint - The Tailings Storage Facility (Domain 7) presents a significant constraint requiring 
detailed, multidisciplinary assessment.
`

### Contamination Report Page 6
`
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
 
 
 
Implementation Approach and Key Recommendations  
A staged, risk-based approach to investigation and management is recommended to support rezoning and 
future development. With appropriate controls and management (including detailed site investigations and 
appropriate remediation), the Precinct and Teralba Southgate can be made suitable for its intended uses 
while protecting human health and environmental values, including Cockle Creek and Lake Macquarie. 
Based on available data and with consideration to identified data gaps, the assessment concludes that 
known or potential contamination is unlikely to preclude commercial/industrial rezoning for the majority of the 
Precinct and Teralba Southgate, provided that targeted investigation and appropriate management controls 
are implemented during future design and construction phases.  However, suitability determinations vary by 
sub-precinct and proposed land use. Employment-led commercial and industrial uses are generally 
considered compatible with the current contamination profile across most sub-precincts. However, more 
sensitive land uses including medium or high-density residential, conservation, and recreational uses, 
require targeted investigation and risk assessment to confirm suitability. This is particularly important at 
Teralba Southgate where detailed ground gas assessments have not been undertaken. The Tailings Storage 
Facility (Domain 7) also represents a material constraint requiring detailed assessment and integrated 
management across multiple environmental and engineering disciplines. 
Implementation of the management framework and performance criteria outlined in this CTA, combined with 
progressive investigation and remediation through the DA approval process, will enable the Precinct and 
Teralba Southgate to support sustainable post-mining land use while protecting human health and 
environmental values in Cockle Creek and Lake Macquarie.  
Based on the findings of this assessment, and subject to the limitations outlined in Section 11, Aurecon 
presents the following recommendations:  
◼ Implement a risk-based, staged investigation program for all future development proposals, with Detailed 
Site Investigations (DSIs) and Tier 2/3 quantitative risk assessments undertaken where elevated COPC 
concentrations are present or data gaps are identified, ensuring land use suitability is confirmed prior to 
development approval 
◼ Prioritise targeted investigation of Domains 2, 5, 7, 14, and 16 (uninvestigated domains), comprehensive 
groundwater assessment across all domains, ASS characterisation in low-lying areas, and asbestos 
delineation sampling to resolve material data gaps constraining sensitive land use suitability 
determination 
◼ Undertake integrated assessment of the Tailings Storage Facility (TSF) across water management, 
geotechnical stability, dam safety, and regulatory compliance frameworks, including geochemical 
characterisation of tailings materials and assessment of leachate generation and Acid Mine Drainage 
(AMD) pathways to inform remediation and long-term management strategy 
◼ Undertake targeted surface water and sediment quality assessments adjacent to waterway and wetland 
interface zones (including recreational areas within West Lake); and assess PFAS-impacted areas in 
accordance with the PFAS National Environmental Management Plan (NEMP 3.1) 
◼ Undertake comprehensive ground gas assessment in accordance with NSW EPA Assessment and 
management of hazardous ground gases (EPA 2020), with particular focus on Domains 11 and 13 where 
methane concentrations were reported. If required, implement ground gas mitigation measures during 
building design and construction to prevent vapour intrusion risk to future enclosed structures 
◼ Confirm land suitability for more sensitive uses (residential in Teralba Southgate, conservation, and 
recreational uses) through targeted investigation of soil, groundwater, surface water, and ground gas; 
implement PFAS plume delineation, asbestos delineation, and methane management measures; obtain 
Site Audit Statements from NSW EPA-accredited Site Auditors where remediation or management 
measures are implemented 
◼ Implement contamination pathway controls during earthworks and future development to prevent 
migration of identified COPCs to Cockle Creek and Lake Macquarie 
◼ Ensure all contamination assessments comply with NSW EPA Guidelines and are prepared by a suitably 
qualified contaminated land consultant including any review and update of duty to report obligations under 
Section 60 of the Contaminated Land Management Act 1997 at each DA-stage investigation and
`

### Contamination Report Page 7
`
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
 
 
 
preparation of Long-Term Environmental Management Plans (LTEMPs) where residual contamination 
risks remain. As per Lake Macquarie City Council’s (LMCCs) requirements, an EPA certified consultant 
must be engaged where reports are submitted to the NSW EPA or where required by the consent 
authority 
◼ All future works should be conducted by an appropriately qualified environmental consultant under the 
direction of a CEnvP-SC or equivalent accreditation. An accredited NSW EPA Auditor may be required to 
endorse the investigation, remediation/management and validation works where more sensitive land uses 
are proposed or where there are elevated risks to ecological receptors. 
Pathway for Residential Development (Teralba Southgate) 
Residential development at Teralba Southgate will require a standard but targeted contaminated land 
assessment and approval process, including: 
◼ Detailed Site Investigation (DSI) involving intrusive assessment to fully characterise the nature and extent 
of contamination across the site in potentially affected media (i.e. ground gas and to a lesser extent soil, 
groundwater, surface water, sediment) with data comparison made to applicable land use criteria as per 
the NEPM (e.g. medium to high density residential). 
◼ Remedial Action Plan (RAP) to define extents and media requiring remediation and/or management to 
render the site suitable for the proposed land uses (e.g. residential). 
◼ Validation sampling and reporting to confirm remediation objectives have been achieved. 
◼ Independent Site Audit by an accredited NSW EPA Auditor which is recommended for residential land 
use on a former mine site, to provide independent assurance of site suitability prior to development 
consent being granted. 
This process will demonstrate that the land is suitable, or can be made suitable, for residential use in 
accordance with the NEPM (2013), EP&A Act, State Environmental Planning Policy (SEPP, Resilience and 
Hazards) 2021 and relevant NSW EPA legislation and guidelines.
`

1 
Introduction 
1.1 
Introduction 
Aurecon Australasia (Aurecon) was engaged by the Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure 
(DPHI) for a variety of engineering services to support the State-led rezoning of the Macquarie Coal 
Complex, herein referred to as “the Precinct” and “Teralba Southgate”. The Precinct and Teralba Southgate 
are located approximately 20 kilometres (km) southwest of Newcastle, in the Lake Macquarie region of New 
South Wales (NSW). It is noted that Teralba Southgate has been removed from the scope of the Master Plan 
and will not be pursued under a State Environmental Planning Policy (SEPP) rezoning process. Instead, 
Teralba Southgate is proposed to be rezoned under a Local Environmental Plan (LEP). A figure showing the 
location and extent of the Precinct and Teralba Southgate is provided in Figure 1, Appendix A. 
The Precinct encompasses the former West Wallsend Colliery (WWC), the Macquarie Coal Preparation 
Plant (MCPP), the former open-cut Westside Mine, and the former Teralba Colliery (Northgate site). Mining 
operations ceased progressively across the Precinct, with Teralba Colliery closing in 2001, Westside Mine 
ceasing operations in 2012, and West Wallsend Colliery and Macquarie Coal Preparation Plant concluding 
operations in 2016.Current activities are focused on long-term environmental management and rehabilitation 
in accordance with the Rehabilitation Management Plan (RMP 2022). 
This Contamination Technical Assessment (CTA) builds on the Post Mining Land Use Engineering Analysis 
Report (Aurecon 2026) and has been developed to support refinement of the Master Plan and to inform 
DPHI's decision making as part of the rezoning process. It is noted that environmental and contamination 
investigations completed to date have been undertaken primarily to support mine closure obligations, rather 
than to establish suitability for alternative land uses. Accordingly, this assessment reinterprets existing 
contamination data within the context of the proposed rezoning and future land use scenarios shown in 
Figure 2, Appendix A. 
1.2 
Objectives 
The objective of this CTA is to support refinement of the Master Plan and to inform DPHI's decision making 
as part of the rezoning process. Specifically, the objectives of this CTA are to: 
◼ Provide a precinct-scale contaminated land evidence base to support rezoning and Master Plan land use 
compatibility, building on existing contamination assessments referenced through Enquiry by Design 
(EbD) engineering discussions 
◼ Confirm whether contamination represents a material constraint to developable land verification for 
employment-led uses and identify where more sensitive land uses would require additional evidence and 
management 
◼ Translate contamination findings into Master Plan-ready objectives, performance criteria and a clear 
staged investigation/remediation pathway to be implemented post rezoning.
`

### Contamination Report Page 11
`
Project number 531168-000  File MCC PMLU Rezoning_Package B_Contamination Report_Final.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B 
 
2 
 
 
 
1.3 
Methodology 
The scope of this CTA comprises five integrated components to achieve the rezoning objectives including: 
◼ Desktop compilation including a review and synthesis of previous investigations and assessment 
documents 
◼ Systematic constraints analysis including the identification of Areas of Environmental Concern (AECs) 
and assessment of remediation implications under different land use scenarios 
◼ Development of a Conceptual Site Model (CSM) to integrate source pathway receptor (SPR) linkages 
across the Precinct and Teralba Southgate 
◼ Identification of data gaps to inform recommendations for future investigation 
◼ Development of a management framework to integrate contamination management into the future 
planning and development approval pathway, including staged investigation triggers, regulatory 
notification protocols, and interfaces with other engineering and environmental disciplines.

## 5. Economic & Market Assessment

### Economics Report Page 5
`
5 
 
Executive summary 
Lake Macquarie City Council engaged Aurecon to undertake an economic, social and market assessment of 
redevelopment opportunities for the Macquarie Coal Complex (Precinct) and Teralba Southgate, collectively referred 
to as the “Site”. The assessment has been undertaken to provide an evidence base for future land use decision-making 
as the Site transitions through closure and rehabilitation towards productive post-mining uses. This report ("Package 
E") brings together baseline demographic, labour market, economic, social and market evidence to support the Master 
Plan process. It tests the land use directions emerging from the Enquiry by Design process and considers their 
feasibility, economic contribution, social implications, market readiness, risks and dependencies.  The assessment is 
prepared for an early planning and pre-investment context. It provides a structured evidence base to support land use 
decision-making, future business case development and further technical testing. 
Market assessment and land use opportunities for employment  
The Site's scale, industrial legacy, proximity to established communities, access to regional labour markets and 
potential infrastructure links favour employment-generating uses. The land use opportunities are grouped into three 
categories. Table 0-1 summarises land use opportunities by priority, time horizon and employment.

### Economics Report Page 6
`
6 
 
The categories above reflect the relative readiness and strategic role of each land use within the Site, having regard to 
market demand, alignment with site attributes, infrastructure and servicing requirements, and the extent of further 
investigation and delivery planning required. These are defined below: 
▪ Highly feasible: circular economy, industrial and advanced manufacturing, waste and resource recovery, and 
energy transition infrastructure and renewables. These uses have near-term feasibility, alignment with the 
Site's scale, industrial legacy and workforce. They can proceed earliest and anchor investment. 
▪ Conditional: freight and logistics, data centres, and community, tourism and agriculture. These uses have 
strategic merit but depend on conditions being met first, principally infrastructure capacity, market maturity, 
anchor tenants and policy support. They are sequenced to later horizons as those conditions are resolved. 
▪ Complementary: residential uses support the Site's function, liveability and public benefit but are not the 
primary economic drivers. 
The Site’s key opportunity lies in its flexibility to accommodate a range of employment-generating, infrastructure-led 
and complementary uses as market, infrastructure and policy conditions evolve. In total, these uses have the 
potential to support around 1,130 ongoing jobs.  
Social implications and consideration for land use  
▪ Minimising social impacts and sustaining social outcomes will depend on how effectively economic benefits 
are accessed, shared and supported over time. 
▪ Transport access is the key consideration across all land uses. Limited public transport accessibility will 
reduce future access to jobs and services, particularly for lower-income households, older residents, people 
with disabilities and those without private vehicles. 
▪ Employment-generating uses (freight, logistics, data centres) offer strong economic potential but introduce 
trade-offs such as increased traffic, amenity impacts and pressure on local services. Industrial and circular 
economy uses align with regional transition objectives, but their social outcomes depend on how well impacts 
are managed and local workforce pathways are enabled. 
▪ Complementary uses (community infrastructure, housing, recreation, health services) are likely to deliver 
more direct social benefits, supporting liveability, wellbeing and place identity.  
▪ Cumulative impacts from workforce growth, concurrent regional activity, and construction and operational 
phases will increase demand for housing and services and require coordinated planning. 
▪ Social outcomes can be strengthened through improved transport access, local employment pathways, 
Aboriginal participation and community benefit sharing. 
Key concluding considerations and next steps  
▪ The Site has credible potential to contribute to Lake Macquarie's economic transition and employment land 
supply and is not immediately market-ready for all uses. Success will depend on staged de-risking, clear 
planning pathways, infrastructure sequencing, targeted market testing and land use flexibility. 
▪ The social findings highlight key considerations which require careful planning, further investigation and 
engagement with the community. Future decisions should not weigh only on the number of jobs or scale of 
investment, but whether benefits are accessible, locally captured and supported by housing, transport, social 
infrastructure and biodiversity outcomes. 
▪ This assessment recommends a pragmatic approach to prioritise feasible employment-generating uses 
and protect longer-term optionality. 
▪ Advance to Business Case development for high priority near term uses, building on this assessment 
through refined technical studies, commercial analysis, and stakeholder validation to support an informed 
investment decision.

1 
Introduction and background  
This Section focuses on project overview, Site, the purpose of the report, strategic alignment and relationship to other 
packages. 
1.1 Project overview 
The Macquarie Coal Complex Pilot Project was initiated as part of the NSW Government’s wider response to the 
Beneficial and productive post-mining land use Parliamentary Inquiry. 4  In July 2025 the NSW Government committed 
to undertake two pilot projects to demonstrate how alternative post-mining land uses can be delivered under the 
existing planning and regulatory framework. 
The Macquarie Coal Complex Master Plan was prepared by the Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure 
(DPHI) in partnership with Lake Macquarie City Council (Council), Net Zero Economy Authority (NZEA) and relevant 
NSW Government agencies involved in post-mining land use, regional development and investment delivery. The 
Department and Council have undertaken master planning informed by technical studies, to support the coordinated 
transition of a former mine site to alternative employment generating land uses in the Precinct 
The Rezoning Proposal applies to approximately 1,160 hectares of land which includes the Macquarie Coal Complex 
Precinct and Teralba Southgate, collectively referred to as the “Site”. 
The Precinct sits adjacent to the Lake Macquarie Northwest Catalyst Area.  It is bounded by the suburb of Barnsley to 
the north, Cockle Creek and the Central-Coast Newcastle main passenger and freight train line to the east, Teralba and 
Wakefield to the south and Killingworth to the west. The M1 Pacific Motorway and Sugarloaf State Conservation Area 
adjoin the western boundary of the Site.   
Teralba Southgate is located at 91 York Street, Teralba, approximately 1.5 km southeast of the Precinct, 400 m from 
Teralba Train Station and 250 m from Booragul Train Station.  
Strategic road and rail connections position the Site to support established and emerging industries and contribute to 
a diverse post-mining economy. The Site also provides opportunities to protect and enhance natural systems, cultural 
and historic assets, and support long-term community and environmental benefit.  
The Site is located in a region with a strong and transferable skills base in mining, heavy industry, logistics, 
construction and land management. As regional mining operations transition toward closure, these skills represent a 
significant asset that can be redirected to support new and emerging industries. 
The Master Plan identifies the arrangement of developable areas, environmentally constrained areas, recreational 
areas, movement networks and key constraints across the Site and outlines the spatial logic for future development at 
a precinct scale, providing a framework within which detailed design, subdivision and development assessment will 
occur.  
The Site is a non-operational mining site located within the Lake Macquarie Local Government Area (LGA) undergoing 
rehabilitation. The Site forms part of a broader regional landscape that is undergoing economic, environmental and 
land use transition as coal related activity declines and mining and energy assets are considered for alternative 
productive land uses. 
The Site has historically supported mining related activity and associated infrastructure. The Site now presents an 
opportunity to consider future post-mining land uses that can deliver economic, social and community benefit while 
responding to rehabilitation, environmental, infrastructure and planning requirements. 
 
4 Cooperative Research Centre for Transformations in Mining Economies (CRC TiME) (2024) Inquiry into Beneficial and Productive Post-Mining Land 
Use, Submission No. 67.

### Economics Report Page 13
`
3 
 
This assessment tests how former mining land can be planned, enabled and transitioned to support employment-
generating land uses, regional economic resilience and broader community benefit. 
 
Figure 1-1: Site context and Master Plan land use areas  
Figure 1-1 shows the Site context and Master Plan land use areas, including developable areas, environmentally 
constrained areas and further investigation areas. 
1.2 Lake Macquarie's strategic role in regional transition economy 
Lake Macquarie is strategically positioned within the Greater Newcastle and Hunter regional economy, with a large 
employment base and strong connectivity to the Port of Newcastle, established freight networks, major transport 
corridors, the Central Coast and Sydney. 5  These locational advantages support the area’s role in the Lower Hunter 
economic system and provide a basis for future employment-generating land uses linked to regional transition. 
The Lake Macquarie community strongly supports the economic transition away from coal alongside a clear 
expectation that redevelopment delivers new jobs, affordable housing, and improved infrastructure.6 Residents place 
high value on environmental quality, public spaces, and overall liveability, while expressing concerns about housing 
affordability, transport connectivity, and involvement in decision-making.7

## 6. Traffic, Transport & Rail Infrastructure

### Traffic Report Page 3
`
Project number 531168-000  File Final_Traffic & Transport_Engineering Report Structure.docx  2026-07-13  Revision C
 
 
 
Executive summary 
This Traffic and Transport Report has been prepared to support the State led rezoning and associated 
master planning of the Macquarie Coal Complex (the Precinct) and Teralba South Gate sub-precinct 
(collectively referred to as the Site), a large post mining landholding west of Cockle Creek in the Lake 
Macquarie Local Government Area. The Site would be located between Teralba and Barnsley, framed by the 
M1 Pacific Motorway to the west, the B89 George Booth Drive and Main Road corridor to the north and the 
B53 arterial to the east. Wakefield Road is the key north-south spine through the area. 
The Site would comprise approximately 211,100 m2 of large format warehousing across the Cockle Creek 
and Killingworth employment cores, and a mixed-use core of around 18,100 m2 at West Lake. At full 
development, the Site is anticipated to generate in the order of 12,470 daily trips of which 1,030 trips are 
expected to be heavy vehicles and 300 RAV 19 metre B-doubles. Peak hour volumes are expected to be in 
the order of 1,400 vehicles in the AM peak and 1,300 in the PM peak. 
Midblock and intersection performance was reviewed across the base year and the short, medium and long 
term, with and without the Precinct and a new Cockle Creek crossing. In the base year the network is 
expected to operate at Level of Service (LoS) A to B, with only the Northville Drive corridor toward the B89 
approaching LoS C to D.  
Background growth is expected to drive the Northville Drive corridor to LoS E by the long term. The southern 
and western network would remain at LoS A to C with the Precinct throughout. The Northville Drive and The 
Weir Road intersections on the northern approach to the B89, would have adjoining links reaching LoS D in 
the short term and level of service F by the long term and consequently have been identified for potential 
upgrade, with proportionate contributions where they reach capacity under background growth.  
Cockle Creek is a major line of severance for access in the wider area resulting in an already congested 
network to the southeast of the precinct. Reducing future vehicle demands on existing creek crossings 
through traffic management, mode shift, infrastructure upgrades and potential new infrastructure will be 
appropriately incorporated within the onward planning for the precinct. 
Three structural findings drive the network strategy: 
◼ The approved RAV B-double network does not extend into the Precinct, and heavy vehicle access would 
need to be newly established. Upgrading The Weir Road would provide the first compliant RAV route in 
the short term, the Wakefield Road route to the M1 would follow in the medium term as the principal 
freight route avoiding Barnsley, Holmesville and nearby schools. RAV access from the east through 
Teralba could be considered in the long term but would likely require a new road and creek crossing to 
bypass the grade constrained Rhondda Road 
◼ Rhondda Road climbs features a sustained uphill climb of approximately 1 km for eastbound traffic with 
grades ranging from 5 to 10%, followed by a sustained downhill descent of over 1 km at similar grades 
with localised slopes over 12 per cent, beyond the Austroads limit for heavy vehicles, so the Precinct is 
not accessible for freight from Teralba by the existing Rhondda Road alignment. Alternative eastern 
access to the Precinct suitable for RAV would be a key part of the strategy, this could be a new Cockle 
Creek crossing or upgrades to other infrastructure that currently restricts RAV access. 
◼ The Five Islands Road crossing of Cockle Creek is the only principal crossing and already operates at or 
near capacity. Therefore, the potential for a second crossing is a consideration in managing Precinct 
traffic in the long term. 
A staged upgrade program has been identified: 
◼ Short term (0 to 10 years). Establish The Weir Road as a RAV 19 metre B-double route to the northern 
access, deliver sub-precinct access intersections, and signalise and upgrade the Northville Drive 
intersections at The Weir Road and the B89 as well as the Northville Drive / Appletree Road / Wakefield 
Road intersection. Establish active transport links and extend public transport connections into the sub-
precincts 
◼ Medium term (10 to 20 years). Upgrade and approve Wakefield Road as the principal RAV 19 metre B-
double route to the M1, upgrade the bridge over the railway in Teralba, deliver improved east-west
`

### Traffic Report Page 4
`
Project number 531168-000  File Final_Traffic & Transport_Engineering Report Structure.docx  2026-07-13  Revision C
 
 
 
connectivity via a new internal road connecting to Wakefield Road, and provide internal spine duplication 
of the Northville Drive corridor. 
◼ Long term (20 plus years). Protect and progress a direct M1 connection, potential reactivation of the 
colliery rail loop with a south facing junction connecting to the Main North Rail Line, railway overpass over 
Racecourse Road upgrade, and integration with the Lower Hunter Freight Corridor and High Speed Rail. 
The assessment concludes that the proposed rezoning is not expected to be the sole contributor to future 
constraints on the surrounding road network, with background growth of local traffic being a key driver of the 
degradation in performance at the key intersections and corridors. Subject to the staged upgrade program 
identified in this report, there are no presently known transport issues that would make the rezoning 
inherently unfeasible.
`

1 
 
 
 
1 
Introduction 
1.1 
Introduction 
This Traffic and Transport Report (this Report) has been prepared by Aurecon on behalf of the Department 
Planning, Housing and Infrastructure (DPHI) to support the State-led rezoning of the Macquarie Coal 
Complex (the Precinct).  This Report assesses the proposed rezoning and accompanying Master Plan in 
relation to traffic engineering and transport planning related matters. 
This Report is structured as set out in Table 1-1. 
Table 1-1: Report structure 
Chapter 
Description 
Chapter 1 – Introduction 
Explains the report's purpose and the methodology used to assess the rezoning. Covers 
the baseline and future horizons, trip generation and distribution, network performance 
approach, and data reliance. 
Chapter 2 – Strategic 
Context 
Sets out the federal, state and local policy and planning framework the report responds 
to. Includes freight, Movement and Place, regional plans and the local LEP, LSPS and 
DCP. 
Chapter 3 – Baseline 
Analysis 
Describes existing conditions across the road, active transport, bus, rail and freight 
networks. Covers crash history, traffic growth, trip patterns, existing midblock 
performance and the key existing traffic issues such as the Rhondda Road gradient and 
severance features for future traffic connectivity such as Cockle Creek and the Main 
Northern railway line. 
Chapter 4 – Structure 
Plan 
Presents the proposed structure plan, transport network and traffic generation, then 
assesses midblock and intersection performance across the horizons. Sets out the freight 
and RAV route strategy, the integration of transport opportunities and the staged required 
upgrades. 
Chapter 5 – Conclusion 
Summarises the baseline results against Precinct findings, a Cockle Creek crossing 
benefit and the staged upgrade program. Concludes the rezoning is feasible subject to 
staged delivery. 
Appendices 
Detailed midblock assessment results supporting the performance analysis. 
1.2 
Purpose of this report 
This Report establishes an evidence based foundation to inform land use planning and infrastructure 
decision making for the Precinct to support the rezoning. The Report has been prepared to: 
◼ Support the development of the Master Plan including the establishment of the movement network 
objectives, access arrangements and connectivity principles, and transport performance outcomes criteria 
aligned with intended land uses. 
◼ Inform State-led rezoning by demonstrating that proposed land use allocations are supported by feasible 
and appropriate access arrangements, and that key transport opportunities and constraints are properly 
integrated into land use planning and staging. 
◼ Identify and prioritise transport infrastructure requirements necessary to enable development, including 
key upgrades and new connections, and to establish indicative timing triggers across short, medium, and 
long-term horizons (0-10, 10-20, and 20+ years respectively), supporting an orderly and coordinated 
delivery of land use outcomes.
`

### Traffic Report Page 9
`
Project number 531168-000  File Final_Traffic & Transport_Engineering Report Structure.docx  2026-07-13  Revision C 
 
2 
 
 
 
1.3 
Methodology 
The assessment follows the TfNSW Guide to Transport Impact Assessment (GTIA) (TS00085 v1.1, 2024), 
and the principles of the TfNSW Transport Management and Accessibility Plan, supported by the Austroads 
Guide to Traffic Management (Parts 3 and 12) and the Austroads Guide to Road Design. The methodology 
applied to each component of the assessment and is set out in the following sections. 
1.3.1 
Baseline and Future horizons 
The baseline and future horizons adopted are as follows: 
Horizon 
Description 
Base year (2026) 
The existing network with current background traffic and no Precinct development. This 
establishes the baseline against which each future horizon is compared. 
Short term (+5 years) 
Background traffic is grown to 2031 and the Precinct is partially developed. Full build 
out is not expected within this horizon and a development reduction factor of 
50 per cent is applied to the generated traffic.  
The key road network connectivity links, being a new Cockle Creek crossing, the 
railway overpass upgrade in Teralba and the proposed internal local road facilitating 
east-west access to the site, are assumed not yet to developed, as well as the 
formalisation of Wakefield Road, south of Rhondda Road as an approved RAV B-
double route. This results in site access to Area 1 being primarily from the northern and 
southern access points via B89. 
Medium term (+15 years) 
Background traffic is further projected to 2041 and the Precinct is assumed to be at full 
build out, with full generation applied (no reduction factor). The connectivity links, 
including a new Cockle Creek crossing, the completion of upgrades to the railway 
overpass, development of the internal east-west local road and approval of Wakefield 
Road, south of Rhondda Road as a RAV B-double route, are assumed to have been 
established, activating the eastern access point to the Precinct.  
Long term (+20 years) 
Full build out and the connectivity links in place, with background traffic further projected 
to 2051. 
1.3.2 
Existing trip patterns 
Existing travel behaviour is established from ABS Census data for 2016 and 2021. Vehicle ownership trends 
are assessed at the Bolton Point to Teralba SA2 level by comparing the distribution of motor vehicles per 
dwelling between the two census years and against the NSW average, indicating the degree of car 
dependency that new land uses must plan around.  
Journey to work (JTW) patterns are assessed from ABS 2021 Census data tabulating place of usual residence 
by place of work at the SA2 level, for the suburbs in and around the master plan area (Teralba, West Wallsend, 
Barnsley, Holmesville, Edgeworth, Fassifern and Wakefield), identifying the dominant modes, the share of 
public and active transport and the principal origin and destination markets. These patterns inform both the 
mode share assumptions and the geographic distribution of light vehicle trips. 
1.3.3 
Background traffic growth 
Background traffic data was drawn from several sources, each covering a different part of the network and 
period. The sources are as follows: 
◼ Draft West Wallsend Rezoning TIA (GHD, 2025), providing 2024 survey volumes at a number of key 
intersections and the STFM (TZP22STMV3.93) growth rates. 
◼ TfNSW permanent classifier stations on the corridors surrounding the study area. Annual Average Daily 
Traffic (AADT) is compiled for each station over the available data periods providing a long‑term dataset 
suitable for understanding historic traffic patterns. with the 2020 and 2021 COVID-19 affected years

table from Transport for NSW Economic Parameter Values (2025), passenger and light vehicles are

Intersection performance 
Intersection modelling was not undertaken for this assessment as base traffic volumes and performance was 
not available, therefore only qualitative assessment was undertaken. For the qualitative assessment, 
approaching midblock LoS were adopted as a guide to suggest intersection performance, as follows: 
◼ Intersections between midblock segments with reserve capacity (LoS C or better) would generally 
operate with reasonable levels of services 
◼ Intersections with any adjoining approach midblock capacity of LoS D or above may be subject to poorer 
LoS D and therefore may require upgrades

## 7. Flooding & Water Management

### Flooding Report Page 3
`
Project number 531168-000  File Final Flooding & WCM Engineering Report_CLEAN.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
 
 
 
Executive summary 
This Flooding and Water Cycle Management Report (This Report) presents a strategic flooding and water 
cycle management (WCM) assessment for the Macquarie Coal Complex Precinct (the Precinct) and Teralba 
Southgate to support a state-led rezoning and Master Plan. The assessment confirms that the Precinct can 
be developed with a coordinated flooding and water cycle management strategy that supports future land 
use while protecting people, property, infrastructure and the surrounding environment.  
The assessment has been undertaken at a master planning scale using available flood studies, water cycle 
data, site investigations and topographic analysis. It focuses on identifying key constraints, opportunities and 
system behaviours rather than detailed design. On this basis, it demonstrates that flooding, stormwater, 
water quality and emergency management outcomes are practically achievable across the Precinct through 
an integrated, precinct-scale approach.  
Flooding Strategy and Precinct Response 
 
Flooding is a defining characteristic of the Precinct, driven predominantly by regional flooding from Cockle 
Creek, supported by local overland flow processes and downstream tidal influences. These mechanisms 
establish the spatial framework for development by identifying where land should be protected, where 
development can occur, and where mitigation is required.  
The Master Plan adopts a fundamental strategy of avoidance first, followed by selective, managed 
encroachment in lower impact areas where development can be supported without increasing risk. This 
approach preserves key floodplain functions while maximising viable development land. 
The recommended flooding strategy is based on several core principles: 
 Preservation of primary floodways and conveyance corridors 
 Retention of flood storage areas and natural drainage pathways 
 Direction of development toward areas of lower flood risk 
 Integration of flood mitigation measures to support safe development 
This approach ensures that development responds to the floodplain rather than working against it, 
maintaining the hydraulic integrity of the system while enabling growth.  
Protection of Surrounding Environment and Property 
A key outcome of the assessment is that the proposed strategy protects both the environmental values of the 
Precinct and the surrounding catchment, while avoiding adverse impacts on adjacent landholdings. 
The Precinct drains to sensitive receiving environments including Cockle Creek, Burkes Creek, Diega Creek 
and Lake Macquarie, which contain important ecological features such as riparian corridors, wetlands and 
key fish habitat.  
The master plan integrates these systems into the development framework by: 
 Maintaining riparian corridors as functional flood conveyance and ecological buffers 
 Protecting water quality through integrated treatment and management measures 
 Avoiding increases in downstream flood risk or changes to flow behaviour 
 Ensuring no material transfer of flood or drainage impacts to neighbouring land 
This ensures that future development does not degrade environmental value or increase risk beyond the 
site, a critical requirement for strategic planning and approval.
`

### Flooding Report Page 4
`
Project number 531168-000  File Final Flooding & WCM Engineering Report_CLEAN.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
 
 
 
Deliverability of Mitigation Within the Precinct 
A significant finding of the assessment is that the majority of required mitigation measures can be 
accommodated within the Precinct footprint. This includes: 
 Floodway corridors and overland flow paths 
 Regional and local detention systems 
 Stormwater conveyance networks 
 Water quality treatment infrastructure 
 Landform adjustments to manage flow and storage 
These measures can be embedded into the structure of future development, allowing flood risk and water 
cycle objectives to be addressed without reliance on extensive off-site works or unacceptable external 
impacts.  
This outcome demonstrates that the Precinct is not constrained in a way that precludes development, but 
instead requires a coordinated and integrated design response at the master planning level. 
Master Plan Intent and Flexibility 
The master plan establishes a clear, achievable and flexible framework for future development. It defines the 
spatial logic, performance objectives and key principles required to guide design and approvals, rather than 
prescribing a single fixed engineering solution.  
Importantly: 
 The strategy is intended to be implemented progressively 
 Future proponents can refine and adapt solutions 
 Any modifications must remain consistent with the overall objectives 
 All outcomes must comply with relevant legislation, policy and performance criteria 
This ensures the plan remains practical and adaptable to future land use changes, evolving design 
approaches and updated technical standards, while maintaining a consistent approach to flood risk and 
water management. 
Demonstrated Feasibility at Strategic Level 
Based on the site review and strategic assessment, all key aspects of flooding and water cycle management 
are considered feasible across the Precinct. 
This includes: 
 Flood risk management and land use compatibility 
 Stormwater collection, conveyance and discharge 
 Water quality management and protection of receiving environments 
 Emergency access and evacuation planning 
While further modelling and design will be required at later stages, the assessment confirms that there is a 
credible and workable pathway to achieve these outcomes within future development.  
Future Opportunities and Sustainable Water Strategies 
In addition to conventional infrastructure, the Precinct presents opportunities to adopt more sustainable and 
integrated water cycle approaches that reduce reliance on traditional systems.
`

### Flooding Report Page 5
`
Project number 531168-000  File Final Flooding & WCM Engineering Report_CLEAN.docx  2026-07-01  Revision B
 
 
 
Potential strategies include: 
 Stormwater harvesting for non-potable reuse at lot or precinct scale 
 Rainwater capture systems to reduce runoff volumes 
 Passive irrigation of landscapes and green infrastructure such as through the use of street trees 
 Distributed bioretention, detention and infiltration, where ground conditions allow 
 Integration of water sensitive urban design (WSUD) across the development 
These strategies can: 
 Reduce demand on downstream drainage infrastructure 
 Improve water quality outcomes 
 Enhance resilience to climate variability 
 Support more efficient and sustainable long-term servicing 
Collectively, they represent a pathway toward a lower impact, more adaptive water management system for 
the Precinct.  
Conclusion 
Overall, the flooding and water cycle management assessment supports a master plan that is practical, 
flexible and environmentally responsible. It demonstrates that development within the Precinct can proceed, 
provided that key floodplain functions are maintained and an integrated strategy is implemented. 
The proposed approach protects surrounding communities and environmental systems, accommodates 
required mitigation within the site, and establishes a robust framework to guide future planning, design and 
delivery.
`

### Flooding Report Page 11

Project number 531168-000  File Flood & WCM Report  2026-07-01  Revision B           1  
 
1 
Introduction 
This report confirms that the Precinct can be planned with a practical and achievable flooding and water cycle 
management strategy that supports future development while protecting people, property, infrastructure and the 
surrounding environment. The assessment has been undertaken at a strategic master planning level and is based 
on a review of the site, available flood studies, water cycle management information, topography, waterways, 
receiving environments and existing infrastructure. On that basis, the overall conclusion is that the key flooding, 
stormwater, water quality and evacuation outcomes for the Precinct are capable of being achieved through a 
coordinated precinct-scale approach, subject to detailed design in later phases. 
Flooding is a defining consideration for the Precinct, particularly in relation to Cockle Creek, its tributaries, local 
overland flow paths and downstream tailwater influences. However, the report demonstrates that these constraints 
do not preclude future land use planning. Rather, they establish a clear framework for where development should 
occur, where flood conveyance and storage should be maintained, and where supporting mitigation is required. 
The proposed flooding strategy is built around preserving key floodways, accommodating regional flow paths, 
providing detention and storage where necessary, and locating future development in areas that can achieve an 
acceptable level of flood risk management. This creates a structure for growth that responds to the floodplain rather 
than working against it. 
A key message of the assessment is that the proposed strategy protects the environmental values of the site and 
the surrounding catchment, while also avoiding the transfer of flood impacts to neighbouring landholdings. The 
Precinct contains and drains to sensitive receiving environments, including Cockle Creek, Burkes Creek, Diega 
Creek and Lake Macquarie. It also contains riparian corridors, wetlands, key fish habitat and other ecological 
values that require protection. The recommended approach maintains these systems as important components of 
the masterplan, allowing them to continue functioning as flood conveyance corridors, ecological buffers and water 
quality protection features. In this way, the strategy is not only about making development possible, but about 
ensuring that the surrounding environment and adjoining properties remain protected from increased flood risk, 
water quality decline or inappropriate drainage outcomes. 
Importantly, the report finds that many of the mitigation measures needed to support future development can be 
accommodated within the Precinct footprint. This includes the retention of floodway corridors, provision of regional 
or local detention, integration of stormwater conveyance pathways, on-site detention, water quality treatment 
systems and landform responses that work with the site’s natural drainage patterns. While detailed sizing and 
alignment of infrastructure will need to be resolved by future proponents, the strategic assessment demonstrates 
that sufficient opportunities exist within the Precinct to embed these measures into future development layouts. 
This is a significant outcome because it shows that the masterplan can establish a viable pathway forward without 
relying on unreasonable off-site intervention or unacceptable impacts to external land. 
The intent of the masterplan is therefore to set an achievable and defensible strategy at a precinct scale, rather 
than prescribe a single fixed engineering outcome. The report makes clear that future proponents may modify 
elements of the ultimate flooding and water cycle response as individual development proposals evolve, provided 
the overall objectives of the masterplan are maintained and all relevant legislation, policy and performance criteria 
are met. This is an important distinction. The report does not seek to lock in detailed design at rezoning stage. 
Instead, it establishes the spatial logic, technical principles and performance expectations needed to guide future 
planning, design and approvals in a consistent way. 
Based on the site review and high-level strategic assessment completed for this report, all core aspects of 
stormwater management, flooding response, water quality management and emergency evacuation are considered 
practically achievable across the Precinct. This does not remove the need for future modelling, detailed design, 
staging reviews and approval pathways. However, it does confirm that there is a credible and workable route to 
resolving these matters through later project phases. In particular, the report shows that flood-compatible land use 
planning, structured drainage networks, protection of overland flow paths, water quality treatment trains and future 
emergency access planning can all be integrated into the development of the Precinct in a practical manner. 
Looking ahead, there is also an opportunity to reduce demand on conventional water cycle management 
infrastructure by exploring more sustainable and integrated servicing strategies as the Precinct develops. These
`



### Precinct Structure Plan Summary
### Page 11

Precinct constraints
The Precinct presents a range of environmental, physical and infrastructure
constraints that are managed through the structure plan and addressed
progressively through staged development and site-specific assessment at the
development application stage.
Geotechnical stability and risk management – Development on the TSF and the
land located in the dam break inundation zone to the north is currently limited.
Soft alluvial soils located around the coastal wetlands in the north of the site
influence construction methods.
Environmental – Existing areas of high biodiversity values, high bush fire risk
and flood affected areas require further investigation to inform development
siting and design.
Sensitive uses – Interfaces between existing land uses, including residential
receivers and potential future industrial land uses will need to be managed to
mitigate negative noise, odour and visual impacts.
Infrastructure – Heavy vehicle access to parts of the site is currently limited
due to steep gradients, access through local residential areas and existing rail
crossings. The location of the existing oil pipeline, electrical transmission lines
and their associated easements, setbacks and design measures will need to be
considered.
Utility infrastructure – Viability and sequencing of the provision of utilities,
particularly to the West Lake sub-precinct, will need further investigation.

Connecting with Country

recognises Country as a living system of
interconnected cultural, ecological and physical

animals, cultural practices, stories and community.
It also recognises that planning for the
transformation of a former mining landscape must
be informed by an understanding of Country and
respectful engagement with Aboriginal people
who hold cultural knowledge, rights and
responsibilities for the area.

3  Macquarie Coal Complex Precinct

### Page 16

Structure plan
The structure plan (Figure 9) is the principal
spatial framework for the draft Master Plan. It
identifies the arrangement of developable areas,
environmentally constrained areas, recreational
areas, movement networks and key constraints
across the Precinct to guide development.
The structure plan outlines the spatial logic for
future development at a precinct scale, providing
a framework within which detailed design,
subdivision and development assessment will
occur. The structure plan is supported by technical
studies.
The following categories are used to describe land
within the structure plan:
•
Developable areas – Land that is potentially
suitable for development, subject to further
technical investigation and site-specific
assessment at the development application
stage.
•
Environmentally constrained areas – Land
identified as having biodiversity values or where
natural features (watercourses, wetlands, steep
slope, flooding) constrain development.
•
Recreation areas – Land potentially suitable for
active and passive recreation facilities,
balanced with protecting biodiversity and
cultural values.
•
Further investigation – Potentially developable
land that is constrained in the short to medium
term by geotechnical, dam safety, biodiversity
or flood risk factors, requiring resolution before
development can proceed. Development of this
land is contingent upon the completion
of relevant technical investigations and the
achievement of applicable staging triggers.

### Page 20

Cockle Creek
Characteristics
Cockle Creek sub-precinct is located in the eastern
portion of the Precinct and encompasses the
former MCPP, TSF, mine rail loop and associated
operational infrastructure. Its direct interface
with the Main Northern Railway, together with
established access from Racecourse Road,
Railway Street and The Weir Road, positions the
sub-precinct as a strategically connected gateway
within the broader Precinct.
Historic industrial use has shaped a substantially
modified landscape of previously disturbed land
and legacy infrastructure. These attributes provide
a strong foundation for early activation and support
the efficient reuse of land.
Key opportunities
The following attributes shape the sub-precinct’s
development context and inform a flexible,
opportunity-led planning response:
•
Reuse of legacy industrial assets – The former
coal preparation plant, mine rail loop and
existing rail interface with the Main Northern
railway provide a platform for new industrial,
manufacturing, energy and rail-related uses that
leverage available infrastructure.
•
Early activation of available land – Areas outside
the TSF and dam break inundation zone are well-
positioned for near-term development, with
further land progressively released as TSF
rehabilitation advances.
•
Enhanced connectivity – A potential new bridge
crossing of Cockle Creek is identified for
investigation to strengthen road access and
unlock additional development opportunities
across the sub-precinct.
•
Improved internal movement networks – New
internal road links will support integrated
movement across the sub-precinct, enabled by
revocation of the TSF dam declaration or
delivery of bridge infrastructure above the
inundation level.
•
Supporting industries that would facilitate rail
manufacturing, including workshops with skilled
trades.
•
Established access for short term activity
– Existing connections from Railway Street,
Racecourse Road, The Weir Road and Rhondda
Road provide immediate access to developable
land beyond the dam break inundation zone,
supporting early-stage uses.
•
Integration of environmental values – High value
biodiversity, fauna habitat and wetland areas to
north and east of the sub-precinct are retained
and integrated into the land use pattern,
supporting a considered balance between
development and environmental outcomes.

Evidence-based sequencing – Geotechnical,
water quality, utilities and access investigations
will inform the staged delivery of development.

Environmentally constrained areas
Characteristics
Environmentally constrained areas form an integral
and defining element of the Precinct’s structure,
comprising tracts of remnant native vegetation,
rehabilitation areas, riparian corridors, waterways
and ecological networks distributed across
the Precinct. Together, these areas establish a
connected environmental framework that supports
biodiversity, water cycle management, landscape
identity and Connecting with Country outcomes.
Environmentally constrained areas anchor the
structure plan in a high-quality landscape setting
and reinforce ecological connectivity across the
region. The intent of these lands is to protect and
enhance environmental, hydrological, cultural and
landscape values over the long-term, while
enabling sensitive land use where suitable.
Key opportunities
The following attributes shape the sub-precinct’s
development context and inform an opportunity-
led, planning response:
•
Anchoring the Precinct’s ecological framework
– High biodiversity areas, key fish habitat
and terrestrial corridors provide north–south and
east–west ecological connectivity across the
Precinct. Biodiversity corridors, coastal
wetlands, mapped waterways and areas subject
to Voluntary Conservation Agreements
informing a clear protection framework aligned
with statutory requirements.
•
Considered, evidence-based approach to
compatible use – Where supported by detailed
biodiversity assessment, low-impact, compatible
uses may be accommodated within these lands,
guided by the recognised hierarchy of avoid first,
minimise where avoidance is not practicable, and
offset residual impacts. This approach makes
sure that future use is sensitively located,
limited in scale, and consistent with the
retention and enhancement of biodiversity,
hydrological and landscape values.
•
Water cycle and landscape performance –
Riparian corridors along mapped waterways
provide for flood conveyance, stormwater
treatment and waterway health, contributing to
a resilient water cycle framework that supports
development outcomes elsewhere in the
Precinct.
•
Compatible low-impact recreation and visitor
experience – Walking trails, lookouts, nature-
based education and interpretation position the
environmentally constrained areas as part of a
layered land use framework that complements
community benefit and economic diversification
across the Precinct.

4  Structure plan and sub-precinct framework

### Page 26

Implementation and sequencing
Implementation
The implementation and delivery of the entire
Precinct vision is intended to be sequenced over
several decades, reflecting the need to manage
mine closure and rehabilitation responsibilities and
to allow the future land uses to evolve in alignment
with development uptake. Delivery of supporting
infrastructure is intended to be staged to correlate
with the development as it is implemented over
time.
Development sequencing provides for
infrastructure delivery that is proactive but demand
led and enables early activation while supporting
long-term, efficient and resilient precinct
development. Infrastructure delivery is to be
adaptive, demand driven and coordinated, ensuring
that development does not outpace enabling
infrastructure while maintaining flexibility for
market led uptake. Development is to be staged in
accordance with available infrastructure capacity,
with priority given to feasibility of connecting
with existing utilities and transport networks.
Infrastructure delivery should adopt an integrated
and coordinated approach across utilities and
transport to minimise disruption and duplication.

4  Structure plan and sub-precinct framework

### Page 29

Short-term activation
Areas identified as being capable of early release
for post mining land use development share
common characteristics, including land where
rehabilitation is at or near completion, existing
road access is available, servicing connections are
feasible and the risk profile is manageable without
requiring major investigations or infrastructure
upgrades.
Early phases are expected to leverage available
capacity within the Argenton Substation, existing
potable water connections and established regional
freight corridors, minimising the need for major
upfront augmentation. These areas also align with
transport accessibility and heavy vehicle routing
capability, supporting early industrial and logistics
uses.
These short-term activation areas provide the
opportunity to demonstrate viability of post mining
land use shift, generate investment confidence and
deliver tangible employment and community
outcomes.
Cockle Creek
Previously disturbed areas outside the TSF and
dam break inundation zone with existing access to
the regional road network. Early activation within
this sub-precinct could leverage the existing rail
loop with opportunities for related manufacturing
facilities.
This sub-precinct benefits from the strategic role
of freight and logistics in this area, leveraging
proximity to supporting flexible, freight-adjacent
land uses as infrastructure staging evolves.
Development in this sub-precinct is staged to align
with infrastructure delivery. A graduated approach
to the staging model is proposed, transitioning
from lighter industry near Cockle Creek to heavier
industrial and warehousing uses further within the
sub-precinct.
Early activation
is subject to geotechnical investigation and
confirmation that development will not
compromise TSF rehabilitation or increase societal
risk within the dam break inundation zone.
Killingworth
The northwest area near Killingworth presents
a strategic opportunity for land uses such as
advanced manufacturing, data-driven uses and
business parks, which take advantage of the
Transgrid substation and existing electricity
capacity. The area offers opportunity for local
uplift and diversification through enabling light
infrastructure, business parks, technology and
data-driven employment to support local jobs and
complement existing residential areas.
The sub-precinct is well-suited for activation given
its previously disturbed land provides a strong
foundation for redevelopment, complemented by
its proximity to existing high-voltage transmission
infrastructure and the Transgrid substation.
Additionally, road access is available via the
existing local road network, with potential for
future upgrades to support new land uses.
Water and wastewater servicing requires further
investigation. Bush fire and biodiversity interface
management will be required along the western
edge.
Barnsley West
Development of previously undisturbed areas
adjacent to existing residential in Barnsley is
contingent upon assessment of transmission
easements, servicing viability and biodiversity
values.
West Lake
The West Lake area offers excellent water quality
suitable for recreational use (subject to ongoing
monitoring). Rehabilitation around the lake has
been progressively signed off, with approximately
one-third approved and the balance targeted
for completion in 2026, indicating suitability for
recreational contact.
Existing walking tracks and connection
opportunities to Sugarloaf State Conservation
Area further enhance the Precinct’s potential for
tourism and recreation uses that are compatible
with the ecological and landscape values of the
area. Basic servicing for visitor amenities would be
required, while the western edge of the lake area
presents ecological constraints including
biodiversity corridors and remnant vegetation that
define the extent of any development boundaries.
Future development around the lake must
maintain water quality through appropriate
catchment management measures.
29
4  Structure plan and sub-precinct framework

### Page 30

Teralba
Teralba sub-precinct is strategically located
between the Teralba and Booragul train stations.
Proximity to existing utility networks reduces
servicing lead times and upfront infrastructure
costs. Established road access and adjacency
to nearby rail stations, residential communities
and employment areas provide a ready platform
for early development. Together, these attributes
present a compelling opportunity to deliver
residential outcomes that complement the
surrounding context and contribute to the
Precinct’s early activation and investment
momentum.
Medium-term activation
Medium-term activation areas require the delivery
of enabling infrastructure, resolution of key
transport and access constraints and progressive
risk reduction through ongoing rehabilitation and
technical investigations. These areas represent the
core employment-generating lands of the Precinct
and will require coordinated investment in roads,
utilities and potentially rail infrastructure.
Progressive development toward the southern and
more constrained areas will require coordinated
upgrades to transport, utilities and Precinct scale
water cycle management. This includes delivery of
wastewater infrastructure (currently absent across
much of the Precinct), expansion of water networks
and potential major electricity augmentation or
new substations where demand thresholds are
exceeded. Concurrently, transport upgrades,
including intersection capacity improvements,
new internal road links and potential additional
crossings of Cockle Creek, must be staged
alongside development to maintain network
performance and resilience.
Cockle Creek and Killingworth
residual land
Previously undisturbed areas outside the TSF and
dam break inundation zone with existing access to
the regional road network. Medium term activation
is subject to biodiversity investigations, geotechnical
investigation and flood risk assessment.
Medium term activation in Cockle Creek sub-
precinct requires confirmation that development
will not compromise TSF rehabilitation or increase
risk within the dam break inundation zone.
Long-term activation
Release of areas identified for long-term
development potential for productive use is
contingent upon the completion of critical
investigations, successful risk reduction through
ongoing rehabilitation and potentially major
infrastructure interventions.
Cockle Creek (TSF and dam break
inundation zone)
Development of the TSF area is contingent upon
several key prerequisites, including the completion
and regulatory acceptance of long-term dewatering
and stabilisation, together with an updated dam
break failure impact assessment reflecting current
capped conditions.
As a declared dam, development in both the TSF
and dam break inundation zone remain limited to
land uses that do not increase the risk to life or
undermine the structural stability of the TSF and
associated structures. The downstream dam break
inundation zone may be reduced in size over time
as rehabilitation progresses, potentially to the
point of revoking its declaration as a dam. Once
the TSF dam declaration is revoked, development
downstream of the dam can be achieved, subject to
other constraints.
Re-purposing and use of the TSF and associated
structures can occur provided detailed structural,
geotechnical and hydrological investigations occur
in parallel to each development application.
Until these prerequisites are met, the TSF and its
associated inundation zone may support interim
or ‘meanwhile’ uses that maintain a low population
level. Such interim uses could include solar energy
installations, for which international precedents on
tailings dams exist, as well as biodiversity corridor
functions, landscape rehabilitation and other low
occupancy uses.
Development within the further investigation areas
associated with the TSF and dam break inundation
areas may be brought forward to medium
horizon, either as a whole or in part, depending on
rehabilitation and ongoing monitoring.

4  Structure plan and sub-precinct framework

### Page 31

Staging triggers
The progressive release of developable land across
the Precinct is linked to the completion of key
technical investigations and the achievement of
rehabilitation milestones. The following staging
triggers apply as relevant across the Precinct:
•
Dam safety and Geotech – Geotechnical
monitoring and assessment of TSF, with the view
to de-declaring the dam. Updated dam break
and consequence assessments demonstrating
a reduction in the dam break inundation zone.
Refer to Section 9 Sustainability and resilience
on page 51 for detailed dam safety aims and
performance criteria.
•
Contamination – Completion of staged
contamination investigations and, where
required, remediation or management
measures appropriate to the proposed land
use, in accordance with the Contaminated Land
Management Act 1997 and State Environmental
Planning Policy (Resilience and Hazards) 2021.
•
Geotechnical stability – Geotechnical
assessments confirming the suitability of land
for the proposed development type, particularly
rehabilitated landforms.
•
Flooding – Detailed flood modelling confirming
flood planning levels, developable areas
and appropriate mitigation measures for
development within or adjacent to flood-
affected land, including climate change and
dam break interaction scenarios.
•
Infrastructure and servicing – Confirmation of
utility servicing capacity (water, wastewater,
electricity, telecommunications) and delivery
of enabling infrastructure to support
staged development. Refer to Section 10
Infrastructure, utilities and servicing on page
58 for detailed infrastructure and servicing
aims and performance criteria.
•
Biodiversity – Completion of detailed
environmental surveys to verify biodiversity
values and confirm environmentally constrained
area boundaries.
•
Take-up and market demand – Progressive
development is informed by demonstrated
market take-up across the Precinct.
The sequencing of development is to be
coordinated with the delivery of supporting
infrastructure to make sure that the transport
network, utilities and community facilities can
accommodate incremental growth. Development
staging should also be coordinated with mine
closure and rehabilitation activities so that land
use transformation proceeds in an orderly manner.
An indicative description of the staging triggers
according to sub-precincts is provided in Table 2.
