4  Structure plan and sub-precinct framework

### Page 33

Desired future
character
The Precinct offers the
opportunity to deliver a
distinctive future character,
shaped by its post mining
landscape and a diverse mix
of land uses. Employment-
generating activity will anchor
the Precinct, complemented
by commercial, recreation,
community and visitor uses.
The Precinct will transform a legacy mining
landscape into the Lower Hunter’s leading post
mining employment precinct, becoming a well-
connected, productive and resilient destination
that is an attractive place to work, invest,
recreate and visit, with high-quality urban form
and integrated landscape.
5	 Built form and landscape
33

### Page 34

The following character principles guide Precinct development.
Built form
The Precinct supports employment generating development responsive to its
landscape context setting. Building orientation, scale and siting is carefully
considered to integrate with the site’s natural features.
Landscape
Building on existing high environmental value, the Precinct protects and
enhances its natural landscape, with remnant vegetation and waterways
treated as defining landscape elements that shape the Precinct’s character.
Biodiversity can be protected near development areas through the
establishment of green corridors, landscape buffers and setbacks that
respond to topography and ecological systems.
Place and identity
The Precinct offers the opportunity to create a distinctive identity that
reflects its natural qualities, cultural values and industrial history.
Public realm and mobility
Well-connected to surrounding networks, the site supports safe, clear
and legible movement systems that connect sub-precincts, support active
transport and prioritise pedestrian amenity and landscape integration at key
interfaces. Access and circulation are designed to respond to the scale of
employment uses.
Sustainability and resilience
Built form and landscape design embed water sensitive urban design
(WSUD), ecological restoration, efficient resource use and climate resilience,
delivering a precinct that performs as a productive landscape.
Interface and transition
Development at precinct edges manages the transition to environmentally
constrained areas and surrounding communities through graduated built
form, generous setbacks and landscape buffers that protect amenity and
maintain natural character.
Flexibility and staged activation
Built form and subdivision patterns accommodate staged delivery and
evolving uses over time, ensuring that interim conditions and early activation
areas contribute positively to precinct character without compromising long-
term outcomes.
34
5  Built form and landscape

### Page 35

Urban design and public realm
Urban design is guided by shift from mining to
employment-generating uses, as well as
opportunities for tourism and recreation. The
design approach prioritises functional, robust
and adaptable built form outcomes that support
a range of industrial, logistics and contemporary
employment uses.
Aims
•
Achieve high-quality urban design outcomes
consistent with the Better Placed (GANSW, 2017)
objectives.
•
Provide a mix of contemporary, high-quality
building types and sizes to support employment
opportunities that evolve in line with changing
economic drivers
•
Built form to be of high quality, with facades
that address the street and that have
articulation, modulation, passive surveillance
and street activation
•
Make sure building siting and scale respond to
landscape, context, environmental values and
interface conditions.
•
Strengthen precinct identity through landscape,
signage, landmarks and heritage-informed
placemaking.
•
Provide landscape setbacks and public realm
outcomes that support amenity, biodiversity and
movement.
•
Create an employment precinct, with well-
connected buildings, commercial nodes and
integrated public and active transport.
Performance criteria
A. Built form, subdivision patterns, open space
and interface treatments must respond to
rehabilitated landforms, engineered surfaces
and the post mining landscape character of the
Precinct.
B. Development must contribute to a coherent
precinct identity by integrating mining heritage,
landscape character and, where relevant,
Connecting with Country outcomes into built
form, landscape and material selection.
C. Buildings must respond to landscape character,
topography and interface conditions.
D. Building scale and height must respond to the
surrounding context, consider visual impact and
contribute to an appropriate variation in built
form character across the Precinct.
E. Road setbacks must incorporate landscape
treatments that strengthen precinct character
and support biodiversity and amenity outcomes.
F.	 Where existing structures are retained, adaptive
reuse must demonstrate compliance with
current safety and accessibility requirements.
G. Public domain, landscape and street design
must integrate WSUD assets to support urban
cooling, biodiversity, amenity and place identity.

5  Built form and landscape

### Page 36

Sub-precinct interface consideration
The Precinct incorporates a range of contextual
interfaces, including environmentally constrained
areas, open space and recreation areas, and
external sensitive receivers such as established
residential communities. Three interface typologies
guide the design of the Precinct’s edges, each
shaped by the values of the adjoining land and
the role of the interface in protecting amenity,
biodiversity and bushfire safety outcomes:
•
Typology 1 – Development adjoining
environmentally constrained areas (including
heavily vegetated and bushfire-prone areas)
•
Typology 2 – Developable area to open space
and recreation areas
•
Typology 3 – Precinct boundary to external
context (including adjoining residential and
other sensitive receivers).
Each typology is illustrated separately in Figure 25
to Figure 27. Together, these treatments establish
high-quality, well-managed edges that retain
the values of adjoining land while supporting the
Precinct’s long-term productive use.
Aims
•
Establish clear, durable transitions between
developable areas, environmentally constrained
areas, open space and surrounding
communities.
•
Protect biodiversity values, ecological corridors,
waterways and cultural heritage from the
indirect effects of adjacent development,
including noise, light, dust, stormwater runoff,
weed invasion and uncontrolled access.
•
Provide that interface built form responds to the
scale, character and amenity of adjoining land.
•
Maintain permeability and connectivity
for people while managing access to
environmentally constrained areas.
Performance criteria
Typology 1 - Development adjoining
environmentally constrained areas
A. Development adjoining environmentally
constrained areas protects ecological values,
manages edge effects, accommodates bushfire
asset protection requirements and supports a
clear, maintainable boundary between urban and
conservation land.
B. A vegetated buffer planted with locally endemic
species must be provided to the environmentally
constrained areas, with a baseline width of 30
metres. This width may be reduced where a site-
specific ecological assessment demonstrates
that a narrower buffer will achieve equivalent or
improved ecological outcomes, having regard to
the sensitivity of the adjoining environmentally
constrained area.
C. Where a perimeter road provides the interface
between developable land and environmentally
constrained area, the road reserve may form part
of the buffer function.
D. The bushland side of any perimeter road must
be managed through bush regeneration to retain
endemic vegetation and ecological values. The
development side of the road must incorporate
low-maintenance bushfire asset protection
zones (e.g. wide road verges, managed grassed
verges) that integrate with the urban edge.
E. Buildings, hardstand, storage and operational
activities must not be located within the
ecological buffer or asset protection zone.
F.	 Development intensity at sensitive environmentally
constrained area interfaces is to be reduced
through setbacks, lower built form scale where
appropriate, and landscape screening to minimise
visual and ecological edge effects.
G. Lighting adjacent to environmentally constrained
areas is to be designed to minimise light spill,
glare and ecological disturbance to habitat areas.
H. Fencing at environmentally constrained
area interfaces must be in the form of a
maintainable barrier that controls access to the
environmentally constrained area while retaining
visual permeability where appropriate.
I.
Stormwater treatment measures must be located
within the developable area and outside riparian
buffers and environmentally constrained areas.
J.
Edge treatments must be designed to prevent
ongoing weed invasion into adjacent native
vegetation, including through selection of
non-invasive landscape species, ongoing
36
5  Built form and landscape

### Page 37

maintenance arrangements and clear physical
demarcation.
Typology 2 – Developable area to open space and
recreation areas
A.	 Development adjoining open space or recreation
areas must provide a positive, legible and active
interface to the public realm.
B.	 Pedestrian access points and landscape
treatments are to integrate seamlessly with
adjoining open space.
C.	 Buildings must be oriented to support Crime
Prevention Through Environmental Design
(CPTED) principles and provide passive
surveillance of public open space, where
appropriate to the proposed use.
D.	 Open space at sub-precinct interfaces are
to function as a connected system that links
environmentally constrained areas, walking and
cycling routes, significant vegetation and natural
landforms.
E.	 Lighting at open space interfaces must support
safe pedestrian movement while applying
CPTED and wildlife-friendly lighting principles
where the open space adjoins or connects to
environmentally constrained areas.
Typology 3 – Precinct boundary to external context
A.	 Development adjoining residential areas or other
sensitive receivers must provide setbacks and
landscape buffers informed by detailed land use
compatibility and acoustic assessment.
B.	 Buildings at sensitive interfaces must be
sited and designed to minimise overlooking,
overshadowing and visual dominance.
C.	 Acoustic, air quality and operational mitigation
measures must be incorporated where required
to manage amenity impacts.
D.	 Heavy vehicle access, loading and servicing
areas are not to be located at residential
interfaces unless no feasible alternative
is available and mitigation measures are
implemented to minimise noise, air quality and
amenity impacts.
E.	 Landscape screening must use locally
endemic species and contribute to amenity and
biodiversity outcomes.
F.	 Boundary interface treatments must respond
to the characteristics of the adjoining land,
including bushfire, ecological, riparian and
residential contexts.

5  Built form and landscape

### Page 39

Biodiversity and habitat corridors
The Precinct consists of a combination of
previously disturbed land, rehabilitation areas and
undisturbed, remnant native bushland. Vegetation
includes existing cleared areas associated with the
historical operation of several former mine sites,
some of which have been rehabilitated back to
native bushland.
Aims
•
Protect areas of high biodiversity values from
development to avoid serious and irreversible
impacts on threatened ecological communities
and threatened species.
•
Promote a development outcome that is
consistent with the requirements of the avoid,
minimise and offset hierarchy of applicable
NSW and Commonwealth biodiversity
legislation.
•
Maintain the function of key linkages that
facilitate wildlife movement within the site and
the wider landscape.
•
Identify and secure environmentally constrained
land with an appropriate size and configuration
to be protected under suitable conservation
mechanisms.
•
Protect wetlands, watercourses, and associated
riparian corridors and aquatic habitats from
development.
•
Implement ecological restoration or
rehabilitation measures on residual land
or buffer zones to achieve a net positive
biodiversity outcome.
•
Apply ecologically sustainable development
principles for future land use and development.
•
Respond and adapt to future environmental
changes to avoid adverse impacts on high
biodiversity values.
Performance criteria
A. Development footprints must be located to avoid
and minimise impacts on areas of high
biodiversity values that could result in a serious
and irreversible impact on threatened ecological
communities or threatened species.
B. Development must avoid fragmentation of
existing key wildlife corridors as identified in the
draft Master Plan and must consider best-
practice species specific guidelines for future
corridor widths.
C. Development must apply Stage 1 and Stage 2
and associated detailed requirements of the
NSW Biodiversity Assessment Method for a
future biodiversity assessment where entry into
the NSW Biodiversity Offset Scheme is triggered.
D. Appropriate buffers and setbacks in accordance
with current applicable NSW and Commonwealth
guidelines must be provided to areas of high
biodiversity value to minimise indirect impacts
and future degradation.
E. Remnant native vegetation must be retained
where practicable and vegetation removal must
be avoided or minimised to maximise tree canopy
cover within a development precinct.
F. Development must incorporate suitable
mitigation measures for restoration or
rehabilitation of native vegetation communities
and associated habitats within residual land
or buffer zones that are enforced under a
Vegetation Management Plan.
G. Rehabilitation of native vegetation communities
and landscape planting must prioritise locally
endemic species and reflect the relevant plant
community types.
H. Development must avoid impacts to wetlands
and associated buffer zones where the wetland
provides important habitat for migratory
shorebirds.
I.
Development must avoid riparian areas and
should restore or rehabilitate the vegetated
riparian zone where practicable.

landscape systems

6  Ecology, water and landscape systems

### Page 41

Water cycle management
Water cycle management is a key consideration
for future development within the Precinct. Diega
Creek and Cockle Creek are the two key waterways
located within the Precinct. Existing stormwater
management within the Precinct is through
natural flow paths within the undeveloped areas
and via water management infrastructure across
disturbed land. Water management infrastructure
includes catchment dams, sediment dams, storage
dams, spillways and drainage systems. Cockle
Creek is the main receiver of stormwater runoff
from the Precinct, receiving flows from upstream
tributaries, natural flow paths and water directed
from the disturbed areas via two licenced locations.
Aims
•
Adopt an integrated water cycle management
approach across the Precinct.
•
Minimise impacts on surface water, groundwater
and downstream receiving environments.
•
Incorporate WSUD to improve water quality,
resilience and place outcomes.
•
Maintain natural drainage function, groundwater
processes and recharge where feasible.
•
Manage construction-phase erosion and
sediment risks to protect sensitive receivers.
Performance criteria
A.	 Development must achieve applicable
stormwater quality targets, including pollutant
load reduction and nutrient load limits relevant
to the Lake Macquarie catchment.
B.	 High-priority groundwater dependent
ecosystems must be protected in accordance
with the applicable policy framework.
C.	 Development must demonstrate an integrated
water cycle and WSUD response that minimises
hydrological and water quality impacts on
downstream sensitive receivers.
D.	 WSUD measures must be designed to treat
frequent storm events, including flows up to and
including the three-month average recurrence
interval (ARI) event.
E.	 Development must maintain, to the extent
practicable, natural flow paths, sub-catchment
function and flow direction and must not rely
solely on piped or heavily engineered stormwater
systems where this would increase erosion,
scour or downstream impacts.
Figure 29: Concept for integrating water sensitive urban design
41
6  Ecology, water and landscape systems

### Page 42

Road network, freight and access
Road network and access is influenced by
the Precinct’s legacy as former coal mine
operations. Historic mining operations have shaped
both the physical terrain and created a framework
that continues to inform movement and access in
the post mining context. Former haul routes and
access tracks, originally designed to support
extraction, processing and transport activities,
establish a foundational spatial logic for the post
mining land use movement network.
The approach to managing transport and mobility is
multi-layered, combining infrastructure planning
and staging through demand management and
integration with land use outcomes. To facilitate
efficient movement within and around the Precinct,
the road network of sub-arterial, collector and local
roads is structured as a clear hierarchy, linking
developable land to the broader regional network
to minimise reliance on the external local network.
Subject to further investigation, a potential new
east–west crossing of Cockle Creek is identified as
an opportunity to strengthen network resilience,
unlock alternative freight access that bypasses
constrained local roads (Racecourse Road, Railway
Street and Rhondda Road) and improve
connectivity to Cockle Creek Station.
Teralba is directly serviced by Teralba and
Booragul Stations and forms a natural anchor for
enhanced pedestrian and cycling connectivity to
the wider Precinct via the Main Northern railway
corridor and the future active transport network.
Aims
•
Provide an efficient, legible and resilient road
network that supports sequenced Precinct
development.
•
Support safe and efficient freight, workforce
and visitor access to and within the Precinct.
•
Align freight and access planning with the
regional road and freight network, including
future strategic corridors.
•
Improve access across existing infrastructure
barriers, including the Main Northern Railway.
•
Integrate flood and bush fire resilience,
emergency access and network safety into road
planning.
•
Deliver complete streets that support walking,
cycling, buses and future transport upgrades.
Performance criteria
A. The Precinct road network must connect
to the regional road network and provide a
legible hierarchy of sub-arterial, collector and
local roads consistent with applicable design
standards.
B. Road network and intersection upgrades must
be delivered ahead of, or concurrently with, the
development stages they are intended to serve.
C. Internal road networks must be delivered
progressively to support sequenced
development and reduce reliance on external
local roads.
D. Development applications must demonstrate,
through detailed traffic assessment, that the
surrounding network and relevant intersections
can operate at an acceptable level of service for
the proposed stage of development.
E. Freight access routes and approved restricted
access vehicle (RAV) connections must be
established before freight-generating land uses
commence.
F.	 Heavy vehicle and freight movements must
avoid residential streets unless no feasible
alternative exists and acceptable mitigation is
demonstrated.
G. Former haul routes and access tracks should
be prioritised for long-term heavy vehicle
movements within and through the Precinct.
H. A freight management plan must be prepared
for freight-generating development and must
address internal freight circulation, last-mile
movements and community impact mitigation.
I.
Roads, intersections and bus facilities must be
designed to provide safe pedestrian access and
support public transport integration.
J.
Internal roads are to be located on developable
land and, where practical, within existing

### Structure Plan Comprehensive Analysis
## Page 11

Precinct constraints
The Precinct presents a range of environmental, physical and infrastructure 
constraints that are managed through the structure plan and addressed 
progressively through staged development and site-specific assessment at the 
development application stage.
Geotechnical stability and risk management – Development on the TSF and the 
land located in the dam break inundation zone to the north is currently limited. 
Soft alluvial soils located around the coastal wetlands in the north of the site 
influence construction methods.
Environmental – Existing areas of high biodiversity values, high bush fire risk 
and flood affected areas require further investigation to inform development 
siting and design.
Sensitive uses – Interfaces between existing land uses, including residential 
receivers and potential future industrial land uses will need to be managed to 
mitigate negative noise, odour and visual impacts.
Infrastructure – Heavy vehicle access to parts of the site is currently limited 
due to steep gradients, access through local residential areas and existing rail 
crossings. The location of the existing oil pipeline, electrical transmission lines 
and their associated easements, setbacks and design measures will need to be 
considered. 
Utility infrastructure – Viability and sequencing of the provision of utilities, 
particularly to the West Lake sub-precinct, will need further investigation.

mining land uses. It establishes the foundation for

Connecting with Country
The Precinct is located on Awabakal Country, 
adjacent to the shores of Cockle Bay in the north 
of Lake Macquarie. The draft Master Plan 
recognises Country as a living system of 
interconnected cultural, ecological and physical 
relationships, including land, waters, skies, plants, 
animals, cultural practices, stories and community. 
It also recognises that planning for the 
transformation of a former mining landscape must 
be informed by an understanding of Country and 
respectful engagement with Aboriginal people 
who hold cultural knowledge, rights and 
responsibilities for the area.

## Page 16

Structure plan
The structure plan (Figure 9) is the principal 
spatial framework for the draft Master Plan. It 
identifies the arrangement of developable areas, 
environmentally constrained areas, recreational 
areas, movement networks and key constraints 
across the Precinct to guide development. 
The structure plan outlines the spatial logic for 
future development at a precinct scale, providing 
a framework within which detailed design, 
subdivision and development assessment will 
occur. The structure plan is supported by technical 
studies.
The following categories are used to describe land 
within the structure plan:
•
Developable areas – Land that is potentially
suitable for development, subject to further
technical investigation and site-specific
assessment at the development application
stage.
•
Environmentally constrained areas – Land
identified as having biodiversity values or where
natural features (watercourses, wetlands, steep
slope, flooding) constrain development.
•
Recreation areas – Land potentially suitable for
active and passive recreation facilities,
balanced with protecting biodiversity and
cultural values.
•
Further investigation – Potentially developable
land that is constrained in the short to medium
term by geotechnical, dam safety, biodiversity
or flood risk factors, requiring resolution before
development can proceed. Development of this
land is contingent upon the completion
of relevant technical investigations and the
achievement of applicable staging triggers.

## Page 20

Cockle Creek
Characteristics
Cockle Creek sub-precinct is located in the eastern 
portion of the Precinct and encompasses the 
former MCPP, TSF, mine rail loop and associated 
operational infrastructure. Its direct interface 
with the Main Northern Railway, together with 
established access from Racecourse Road, 
Railway Street and The Weir Road, positions the 
sub-precinct as a strategically connected gateway 
within the broader Precinct. 
Historic industrial use has shaped a substantially 
modified landscape of previously disturbed land 
and legacy infrastructure. These attributes provide 
a strong foundation for early activation and support 
the efficient reuse of land.
Key opportunities
The following attributes shape the sub-precinct’s 
development context and inform a flexible, 
opportunity-led planning response:
•
Reuse of legacy industrial assets – The former 
coal preparation plant, mine rail loop and 
existing rail interface with the Main Northern 
railway provide a platform for new industrial, 
manufacturing, energy and rail-related uses that 
leverage available infrastructure. 
•
Early activation of available land – Areas outside 
the TSF and dam break inundation zone are well-
positioned for near-term development, with 
further land progressively released as TSF 
rehabilitation advances.
•
Enhanced connectivity – A potential new bridge 
crossing of Cockle Creek is identified for 
investigation to strengthen road access and 
unlock additional development opportunities 
across the sub-precinct.
•
Improved internal movement networks – New 
internal road links will support integrated 
movement across the sub-precinct, enabled by 
revocation of the TSF dam declaration or 
delivery of bridge infrastructure above the 
inundation level.
•
Supporting industries that would facilitate rail 
manufacturing, including workshops with skilled 
trades.
•
Established access for short term activity
– Existing connections from Railway Street,
Racecourse Road, The Weir Road and Rhondda
Road provide immediate access to developable
land beyond the dam break inundation zone,
supporting early-stage uses.
•
Integration of environmental values – High value
biodiversity, fauna habitat and wetland areas to
north and east of the sub-precinct are retained
and integrated into the land use pattern,
supporting a considered balance between
development and environmental outcomes.

## Page 25

Environmentally constrained areas
Characteristics
Environmentally constrained areas form an integral 
and defining element of the Precinct’s structure, 
comprising tracts of remnant native vegetation, 
rehabilitation areas, riparian corridors, waterways 
and ecological networks distributed across 
the Precinct. Together, these areas establish a 
connected environmental framework that supports 
biodiversity, water cycle management, landscape 
identity and Connecting with Country outcomes.
Environmentally constrained areas anchor the 
structure plan in a high-quality landscape setting 
and reinforce ecological connectivity across the 
region. The intent of these lands is to protect and 
enhance environmental, hydrological, cultural and 
landscape values over the long-term, while 
enabling sensitive land use where suitable.
Key opportunities
The following attributes shape the sub-precinct’s 
development context and inform an opportunity-
led, planning response:
•
Anchoring the Precinct’s ecological framework
– High biodiversity areas, key fish habitat
and terrestrial corridors provide north–south and 
east–west ecological connectivity across the 
Precinct. Biodiversity corridors, coastal 
wetlands, mapped waterways and areas subject 
to Voluntary Conservation Agreements 
informing a clear protection framework aligned 
with statutory requirements.
•
Considered, evidence-based approach to 
compatible use – Where supported by detailed 
biodiversity assessment, low-impact, compatible 
uses may be accommodated within these lands, 
guided by the recognised hierarchy of avoid first, 
minimise where avoidance is not practicable, and 
offset residual impacts. This approach makes 
sure that future use is sensitively located, 
limited in scale, and consistent with the 
retention and enhancement of biodiversity, 
hydrological and landscape values.
•
Water cycle and landscape performance –
Riparian corridors along mapped waterways
provide for flood conveyance, stormwater 
treatment and waterway health, contributing to 
a resilient water cycle framework that supports 
development outcomes elsewhere in the 
Precinct.
•
Compatible low-impact recreation and visitor
experience – Walking trails, lookouts, nature-
based education and interpretation position the
environmentally constrained areas as part of a
layered land use framework that complements
community benefit and economic diversification
across the Precinct.

## Page 26

Implementation and sequencing
Implementation
The implementation and delivery of the entire 
Precinct vision is intended to be sequenced over 
several decades, reflecting the need to manage 
mine closure and rehabilitation responsibilities and 
to allow the future land uses to evolve in alignment 
with development uptake. Delivery of supporting 
infrastructure is intended to be staged to correlate 
with the development as it is implemented over 
time.
Development sequencing provides for 
infrastructure delivery that is proactive but demand 
led and enables early activation while supporting 
long-term, efficient and resilient precinct 
development. Infrastructure delivery is to be 
adaptive, demand driven and coordinated, ensuring 
that development does not outpace enabling 
infrastructure while maintaining flexibility for 
market led uptake. Development is to be staged in 
accordance with available infrastructure capacity, 
with priority given to feasibility of connecting 
with existing utilities and transport networks. 
Infrastructure delivery should adopt an integrated 
and coordinated approach across utilities and 
transport to minimise disruption and duplication.

## Page 29

Short-term activation
Areas identified as being capable of early release 
for post mining land use development share 
common characteristics, including land where 
rehabilitation is at or near completion, existing 
road access is available, servicing connections are 
feasible and the risk profile is manageable without 
requiring major investigations or infrastructure 
upgrades.
Early phases are expected to leverage available 
capacity within the Argenton Substation, existing 
potable water connections and established regional 
freight corridors, minimising the need for major 
upfront augmentation. These areas also align with 
transport accessibility and heavy vehicle routing 
capability, supporting early industrial and logistics 
uses.
These short-term activation areas provide the 
opportunity to demonstrate viability of post mining 
land use shift, generate investment confidence and 
deliver tangible employment and community 
outcomes.
Cockle Creek
Previously disturbed areas outside the TSF and 
dam break inundation zone with existing access to 
the regional road network. Early activation within 
this sub-precinct could leverage the existing rail 
loop with opportunities for related manufacturing 
facilities.
This sub-precinct benefits from the strategic role 
of freight and logistics in this area, leveraging 
proximity to supporting flexible, freight-adjacent 
land uses as infrastructure staging evolves.
Development in this sub-precinct is staged to align 
with infrastructure delivery. A graduated approach 
to the staging model is proposed, transitioning 
from lighter industry near Cockle Creek to heavier 
industrial and warehousing uses further within the 
sub-precinct. 
Early activation 
is subject to geotechnical investigation and 
confirmation that development will not 
compromise TSF rehabilitation or increase societal 
risk within the dam break inundation zone.
Killingworth
The northwest area near Killingworth presents 
a strategic opportunity for land uses such as 
advanced manufacturing, data-driven uses and 
business parks, which take advantage of the 
Transgrid substation and existing electricity 
capacity. The area offers opportunity for local 
uplift and diversification through enabling light 
infrastructure, business parks, technology and 
data-driven employment to support local jobs and 
complement existing residential areas.
The sub-precinct is well-suited for activation given 
its previously disturbed land provides a strong 
foundation for redevelopment, complemented by 
its proximity to existing high-voltage transmission 
infrastructure and the Transgrid substation. 
Additionally, road access is available via the 
existing local road network, with potential for 
future upgrades to support new land uses.
Water and wastewater servicing requires further 
investigation. Bush fire and biodiversity interface 
management will be required along the western 
edge.
Barnsley West 
Development of previously undisturbed areas 
adjacent to existing residential in Barnsley is 
contingent upon assessment of transmission 
easements, servicing viability and biodiversity 
values.
West Lake
The West Lake area offers excellent water quality 
suitable for recreational use (subject to ongoing 
monitoring). Rehabilitation around the lake has 
been progressively signed off, with approximately 
one-third approved and the balance targeted 
for completion in 2026, indicating suitability for 
recreational contact. 
Existing walking tracks and connection 
opportunities to Sugarloaf State Conservation 
Area further enhance the Precinct’s potential for 
tourism and recreation uses that are compatible 
with the ecological and landscape values of the 
area. Basic servicing for visitor amenities would be 
required, while the western edge of the lake area 
presents ecological constraints including 
biodiversity corridors and remnant vegetation that 
define the extent of any development boundaries. 
Future development around the lake must 
maintain water quality through appropriate 
catchment management measures.

## Page 30

Teralba
Teralba sub-precinct is strategically located 
between the Teralba and Booragul train stations. 
Proximity to existing utility networks reduces 
servicing lead times and upfront infrastructure 
costs. Established road access and adjacency 
to nearby rail stations, residential communities 
and employment areas provide a ready platform 
for early development. Together, these attributes 
present a compelling opportunity to deliver 
residential outcomes that complement the 
surrounding context and contribute to the 
Precinct’s early activation and investment 
momentum.
Medium-term activation
Medium-term activation areas require the delivery 
of enabling infrastructure, resolution of key 
transport and access constraints and progressive 
risk reduction through ongoing rehabilitation and 
technical investigations. These areas represent the 
core employment-generating lands of the Precinct 
and will require coordinated investment in roads, 
utilities and potentially rail infrastructure.
Progressive development toward the southern and 
more constrained areas will require coordinated 
upgrades to transport, utilities and Precinct scale 
water cycle management. This includes delivery of 
wastewater infrastructure (currently absent across 
much of the Precinct), expansion of water networks 
and potential major electricity augmentation or 
new substations where demand thresholds are 
exceeded. Concurrently, transport upgrades, 
including intersection capacity improvements, 
new internal road links and potential additional 
crossings of Cockle Creek, must be staged 
alongside development to maintain network 
performance and resilience.
Cockle Creek and Killingworth 
residual land
Previously undisturbed areas outside the TSF and 
dam break inundation zone with existing access to 
the regional road network. Medium term activation 
is subject to biodiversity investigations, geotechnical 
investigation and flood risk assessment. 
Medium term activation in Cockle Creek sub-
precinct requires confirmation that development 
will not compromise TSF rehabilitation or increase 
risk within the dam break inundation zone. 
Long-term activation
Release of areas identified for long-term 
development potential for productive use is 
contingent upon the completion of critical 
investigations, successful risk reduction through 
ongoing rehabilitation and potentially major 
infrastructure interventions. 
Cockle Creek (TSF and dam break 
inundation zone)
Development of the TSF area is contingent upon 
several key prerequisites, including the completion 
and regulatory acceptance of long-term dewatering 
and stabilisation, together with an updated dam 
break failure impact assessment reflecting current 
capped conditions. 
As a declared dam, development in both the TSF 
and dam break inundation zone remain limited to 
land uses that do not increase the risk to life or 
undermine the structural stability of the TSF and 
associated structures. The downstream dam break 
inundation zone may be reduced in size over time 
as rehabilitation progresses, potentially to the 
point of revoking its declaration as a dam. Once 
the TSF dam declaration is revoked, development 
downstream of the dam can be achieved, subject to 
other constraints. 
Re-purposing and use of the TSF and associated 
structures can occur provided detailed structural, 
geotechnical and hydrological investigations occur 
in parallel to each development application.
Until these prerequisites are met, the TSF and its 
associated inundation zone may support interim 
or ‘meanwhile’ uses that maintain a low population 
level. Such interim uses could include solar energy 
installations, for which international precedents on 
tailings dams exist, as well as biodiversity corridor 
functions, landscape rehabilitation and other low 
occupancy uses.
Development within the further investigation areas 
associated with the TSF and dam break inundation 
areas may be brought forward to medium 
horizon, either as a whole or in part, depending on 
rehabilitation and ongoing monitoring.
30

## Page 31

Staging triggers
The progressive release of developable land across 
the Precinct is linked to the completion of key 
technical investigations and the achievement of 
rehabilitation milestones. The following staging 
triggers apply as relevant across the Precinct:
•
Dam safety and Geotech – Geotechnical
monitoring and assessment of TSF, with the view
to de-declaring the dam. Updated dam break
and consequence assessments demonstrating
a reduction in the dam break inundation zone.
Refer to Section 9 Sustainability and resilience
on page 51 for detailed dam safety aims and
performance criteria.
•
Contamination – Completion of staged
contamination investigations and, where
required, remediation or management
measures appropriate to the proposed land
use, in accordance with the Contaminated Land
Management Act 1997 and State Environmental
Planning Policy (Resilience and Hazards) 2021.
•
Geotechnical stability – Geotechnical
assessments confirming the suitability of land
for the proposed development type, particularly
rehabilitated landforms.
•
Flooding – Detailed flood modelling confirming
flood planning levels, developable areas
and appropriate mitigation measures for
development within or adjacent to flood-
affected land, including climate change and
dam break interaction scenarios.
•
Infrastructure and servicing – Confirmation of
utility servicing capacity (water, wastewater,
electricity, telecommunications) and delivery
of enabling infrastructure to support
staged development. Refer to Section 10
Infrastructure, utilities and servicing on page
58 for detailed infrastructure and servicing
aims and performance criteria.
•
Biodiversity – Completion of detailed
environmental surveys to verify biodiversity
values and confirm environmentally constrained
area boundaries.
•
Take-up and market demand – Progressive
development is informed by demonstrated
market take-up across the Precinct.
The sequencing of development is to be 
coordinated with the delivery of supporting 
infrastructure to make sure that the transport 
network, utilities and community facilities can 
accommodate incremental growth. Development 
staging should also be coordinated with mine 
closure and rehabilitation activities so that land 
use transformation proceeds in an orderly manner. 
An indicative description of the staging triggers 
according to sub-precincts is provided in Table 2.

## Page 33

Desired future 
character
The Precinct offers the 
opportunity to deliver a 
distinctive future character, 
shaped by its post mining 
landscape and a diverse mix 
of land uses. Employment-
generating activity will anchor 
the Precinct, complemented 
by commercial, recreation, 
community and visitor uses.
The Precinct will transform a legacy mining 
landscape into the Lower Hunter’s leading post 
mining employment precinct, becoming a well-
connected, productive and resilient destination 
that is an attractive place to work, invest, 
recreate and visit, with high-quality urban form 
and integrated landscape.
5	 Built form and landscape
33



## Page 34

 The following character principles guide Precinct development.
Built form
The Precinct supports employment generating development responsive to its 
landscape context setting. Building orientation, scale and siting is carefully 
considered to integrate with the site’s natural features. 
Landscape
Building on existing high environmental value, the Precinct protects and 
enhances its natural landscape, with remnant vegetation and waterways 
treated as defining landscape elements that shape the Precinct’s character. 
Biodiversity can be protected near development areas through the 
establishment of green corridors, landscape buffers and setbacks that 
respond to topography and ecological systems. 
Place and identity
The Precinct offers the opportunity to create a distinctive identity that 
reflects its natural qualities, cultural values and industrial history. 
Public realm and mobility
Well-connected to surrounding networks, the site supports safe, clear 
and legible movement systems that connect sub-precincts, support active 
transport and prioritise pedestrian amenity and landscape integration at key 
interfaces. Access and circulation are designed to respond to the scale of 
employment uses.
Sustainability and resilience
Built form and landscape design embed water sensitive urban design 
(WSUD), ecological restoration, efficient resource use and climate resilience, 
delivering a precinct that performs as a productive landscape.
Interface and transition
Development at precinct edges manages the transition to environmentally 
constrained areas and surrounding communities through graduated built 
form, generous setbacks and landscape buffers that protect amenity and 
maintain natural character.
Flexibility and staged activation
Built form and subdivision patterns accommodate staged delivery and 
evolving uses over time, ensuring that interim conditions and early activation 
areas contribute positively to precinct character without compromising long-
term outcomes.
34

## Page 36

Sub-precinct interface consideration
The Precinct incorporates a range of contextual 
interfaces, including environmentally constrained 
areas, open space and recreation areas, and 
external sensitive receivers such as established 
residential communities. Three interface typologies 
guide the design of the Precinct’s edges, each 
shaped by the values of the adjoining land and 
the role of the interface in protecting amenity, 
biodiversity and bushfire safety outcomes:
•
Typology 1 – Development adjoining
environmentally constrained areas (including
heavily vegetated and bushfire-prone areas)
•
Typology 2 – Developable area to open space
and recreation areas
•
Typology 3 – Precinct boundary to external
context (including adjoining residential and
other sensitive receivers).
Each typology is illustrated separately in Figure 25 
to Figure 27. Together, these treatments establish 
high-quality, well-managed edges that retain 
the values of adjoining land while supporting the 
Precinct’s long-term productive use.
Aims
•
Establish clear, durable transitions between 
developable areas, environmentally constrained 
areas, open space and surrounding 
communities.
•
Protect biodiversity values, ecological corridors, 
waterways and cultural heritage from the 
indirect effects of adjacent development, 
including noise, light, dust, stormwater runoff, 
weed invasion and uncontrolled access.
•
Provide that interface built form responds to the 
scale, character and amenity of adjoining land.
•
Maintain permeability and connectivity
for people while managing access to 
environmentally constrained areas.
Performance criteria
Typology 1 - Development adjoining 
environmentally constrained areas
A. Development adjoining environmentally
constrained areas protects ecological values,
manages edge effects, accommodates bushfire
asset protection requirements and supports a
clear, maintainable boundary between urban and 
conservation land.
B. A vegetated buffer planted with locally endemic
species must be provided to the environmentally
constrained areas, with a baseline width of 30
metres. This width may be reduced where a site-
specific ecological assessment demonstrates
that a narrower buffer will achieve equivalent or
improved ecological outcomes, having regard to
the sensitivity of the adjoining environmentally
constrained area.
C. Where a perimeter road provides the interface
between developable land and environmentally
constrained area, the road reserve may form part
of the buffer function.
D. The bushland side of any perimeter road must
be managed through bush regeneration to retain
endemic vegetation and ecological values. The
development side of the road must incorporate
low-maintenance bushfire asset protection
zones (e.g. wide road verges, managed grassed
verges) that integrate with the urban edge.
E. Buildings, hardstand, storage and operational
activities must not be located within the
ecological buffer or asset protection zone.
F.	 Development intensity at sensitive environmentally
constrained area interfaces is to be reduced
through setbacks, lower built form scale where
appropriate, and landscape screening to minimise
visual and ecological edge effects.
G. Lighting adjacent to environmentally constrained
areas is to be designed to minimise light spill,
glare and ecological disturbance to habitat areas.
H. Fencing at environmentally constrained
area interfaces must be in the form of a
maintainable barrier that controls access to the
environmentally constrained area while retaining
visual permeability where appropriate.
I.
Stormwater treatment measures must be located
within the developable area and outside riparian
buffers and environmentally constrained areas.
J.
Edge treatments must be designed to prevent
ongoing weed invasion into adjacent native
vegetation, including through selection of
non-invasive landscape species, ongoing

demarcation.

## Page 39

Biodiversity and habitat corridors
The Precinct consists of a combination of 
previously disturbed land, rehabilitation areas and 
undisturbed, remnant native bushland. Vegetation 
includes existing cleared areas associated with the 
historical operation of several former mine sites, 
some of which have been rehabilitated back to 
native bushland. 
Aims
•
Protect areas of high biodiversity values from
development to avoid serious and irreversible
impacts on threatened ecological communities
and threatened species.
•
Promote a development outcome that is
consistent with the requirements of the avoid,
minimise and offset hierarchy of applicable
NSW and Commonwealth biodiversity
legislation.
•
Maintain the function of key linkages that
facilitate wildlife movement within the site and
the wider landscape.
•
Identify and secure environmentally constrained
land with an appropriate size and configuration
to be protected under suitable conservation
mechanisms.
•
Protect wetlands, watercourses, and associated
riparian corridors and aquatic habitats from
development.
•
Implement ecological restoration or
rehabilitation measures on residual land
or buffer zones to achieve a net positive
biodiversity outcome.
•
Apply ecologically sustainable development
principles for future land use and development.
•
Respond and adapt to future environmental
changes to avoid adverse impacts on high
biodiversity values.
Performance criteria
A. Development footprints must be located to avoid
and minimise impacts on areas of high
biodiversity values that could result in a serious
and irreversible impact on threatened ecological
communities or threatened species.
B. Development must avoid fragmentation of
existing key wildlife corridors as identified in the
draft Master Plan and must consider best-
practice species specific guidelines for future
corridor widths.
C. Development must apply Stage 1 and Stage 2
and associated detailed requirements of the
NSW Biodiversity Assessment Method for a
future biodiversity assessment where entry into
the NSW Biodiversity Offset Scheme is triggered.
D. Appropriate buffers and setbacks in accordance
with current applicable NSW and Commonwealth
guidelines must be provided to areas of high
biodiversity value to minimise indirect impacts
and future degradation.
E. Remnant native vegetation must be retained
where practicable and vegetation removal must
be avoided or minimised to maximise tree canopy
cover within a development precinct.
F. Development must incorporate suitable
mitigation measures for restoration or
rehabilitation of native vegetation communities
and associated habitats within residual land
or buffer zones that are enforced under a
Vegetation Management Plan.
G. Rehabilitation of native vegetation communities
and landscape planting must prioritise locally
endemic species and reflect the relevant plant
community types.
H. Development must avoid impacts to wetlands
and associated buffer zones where the wetland
provides important habitat for migratory
shorebirds.
I.
Development must avoid riparian areas and
should restore or rehabilitate the vegetated
riparian zone where practicable.

landscape systems

6  Ecology, water and landscape systems



## Page 41

Water cycle management
Water cycle management is a key consideration 
for future development within the Precinct. Diega 
Creek and Cockle Creek are the two key waterways 
located within the Precinct. Existing stormwater 
management within the Precinct is through 
natural flow paths within the undeveloped areas 
and via water management infrastructure across 
disturbed land. Water management infrastructure 
includes catchment dams, sediment dams, storage 
dams, spillways and drainage systems. Cockle 
Creek is the main receiver of stormwater runoff 
from the Precinct, receiving flows from upstream 
tributaries, natural flow paths and water directed 
from the disturbed areas via two licenced locations. 
Aims
	•
Adopt an integrated water cycle management 
approach across the Precinct.
	•
Minimise impacts on surface water, groundwater 
and downstream receiving environments.
	•
Incorporate WSUD to improve water quality, 
resilience and place outcomes.
	•
Maintain natural drainage function, groundwater 
processes and recharge where feasible.
	•
Manage construction-phase erosion and 
sediment risks to protect sensitive receivers. 
Performance criteria
A.	 Development must achieve applicable 
stormwater quality targets, including pollutant 
load reduction and nutrient load limits relevant 
to the Lake Macquarie catchment.
B.	 High-priority groundwater dependent 
ecosystems must be protected in accordance 
with the applicable policy framework.
C.	 Development must demonstrate an integrated 
water cycle and WSUD response that minimises 
hydrological and water quality impacts on 
downstream sensitive receivers.
D.	 WSUD measures must be designed to treat 
frequent storm events, including flows up to and 
including the three-month average recurrence 
interval (ARI) event.
E.	 Development must maintain, to the extent 
practicable, natural flow paths, sub-catchment 
function and flow direction and must not rely 
solely on piped or heavily engineered stormwater 
systems where this would increase erosion, 
scour or downstream impacts.

Road network, freight and access
Road network and access is influenced by 
the Precinct’s legacy as former coal mine 
operations. Historic mining operations have shaped 
both the physical terrain and created a framework 
that continues to inform movement and access in 
the post mining context. Former haul routes and 
access tracks, originally designed to support 
extraction, processing and transport activities, 
establish a foundational spatial logic for the post 
mining land use movement network. 
The approach to managing transport and mobility is 
multi-layered, combining infrastructure planning 
and staging through demand management and 
integration with land use outcomes. To facilitate 
efficient movement within and around the Precinct, 
the road network of sub-arterial, collector and local 
roads is structured as a clear hierarchy, linking 
developable land to the broader regional network 
to minimise reliance on the external local network. 
Subject to further investigation, a potential new 
east–west crossing of Cockle Creek is identified as 
an opportunity to strengthen network resilience, 
unlock alternative freight access that bypasses 
constrained local roads (Racecourse Road, Railway 
Street and Rhondda Road) and improve 
connectivity to Cockle Creek Station.
Teralba is directly serviced by Teralba and 
Booragul Stations and forms a natural anchor for 
enhanced pedestrian and cycling connectivity to 
the wider Precinct via the Main Northern railway 
corridor and the future active transport network.
Aims
•
Provide an efficient, legible and resilient road
network that supports sequenced Precinct
development.
•
Support safe and efficient freight, workforce
and visitor access to and within the Precinct.
•
Align freight and access planning with the
regional road and freight network, including
future strategic corridors.
•
Improve access across existing infrastructure
barriers, including the Main Northern Railway.
•
Integrate flood and bush fire resilience,
emergency access and network safety into road
planning.
•
Deliver complete streets that support walking,
cycling, buses and future transport upgrades.
Performance criteria
A. The Precinct road network must connect
to the regional road network and provide a
legible hierarchy of sub-arterial, collector and
local roads consistent with applicable design
standards.
B. Road network and intersection upgrades must
be delivered ahead of, or concurrently with, the
development stages they are intended to serve.
C. Internal road networks must be delivered
progressively to support sequenced
development and reduce reliance on external
local roads.
D. Development applications must demonstrate,
through detailed traffic assessment, that the
surrounding network and relevant intersections
can operate at an acceptable level of service for
the proposed stage of development.
E. Freight access routes and approved restricted
access vehicle (RAV) connections must be
established before freight-generating land uses
commence.
F.	 Heavy vehicle and freight movements must
avoid residential streets unless no feasible
alternative exists and acceptable mitigation is
demonstrated.
G. Former haul routes and access tracks should
be prioritised for long-term heavy vehicle
movements within and through the Precinct.
H. A freight management plan must be prepared
for freight-generating development and must
address internal freight circulation, last-mile
movements and community impact mitigation.
I.
Roads, intersections and bus facilities must be
designed to provide safe pedestrian access and
support public transport integration.
J.
Internal roads are to be located on developable
land and, where practical, within existing

# Part III: Strategic Government Value & Benefits Realization Guide

# Strategic Value & Application Guide: Transferring Cloud Spatial Lakehouse Lessons to NSW Government & Waratah HPC

**Target Audience:** Senior Geospatial Leadership, Spatial Data Architects, HPC Systems Engineers & Policy Executives (NSW DCCEEW, NSW Spatial Services, Department of Planning, Housing and Infrastructure – DPHI, EnergyCo, Environment & Heritage, and Land Administration)  
**Procurement Reference Context:** [NSW Buy Notice C85F11C0-9984-4C2A-AE897364B13F4B7A](https://buy.nsw.gov.au/notices/C85F11C0-9984-4C2A-AE897364B13F4B7A) (NSW DCCEEW Waratah HPC Facility)  
**Reference Portals:** [NSW Spatial Services](https://www.spatial.nsw.gov.au/) | [NSW Planning Portal](https://www.planningportal.nsw.gov.au/) | [NSW SEED Portal](https://www.seed.nsw.gov.au/)  
**Document Purpose:** Strategic and technical transfer guide detailing how the architectural patterns, cost optimizations, and distributed spatial ETL lessons developed in **AURA Siting Crafter** (deployed on Wherobots Cloud, AWS, and Google Cloud) can be directly applied to accelerate on-premise government supercomputing—specifically the **NSW DCCEEW Waratah High Performance Computing (HPC) Facility** and enterprise state GIS platforms.

---

> [!IMPORTANT]
> ### ⚡ Executive Briefing: Cloud-to-HPC Architectural Transfer
> * **Cloud-Native Provenance:** AURA Siting Crafter executes natively on **Wherobots Cloud, AWS (S3 / EMR / Sedona), and Google Cloud (BigQuery / GCS)**, processing 15.91M+ national spatial geometries with statutory multi-hazard scoring.
> * **The Core Objective:** Translating cloud spatial engineering breakthroughs—distributed Apache Sedona memory models, decoupled geometry/scoring engines, and GeoParquet lakehouse structures—into concrete design patterns for NSW Government compute environments, notably **Waratah HPC** ([buy.nsw Notice C85F11C0-9984-4C2A-AE897364B13F4B7A](https://buy.nsw.gov.au/notices/C85F11C0-9984-4C2A-AE897364B13F4B7A)).
> * **Lustre Parallel Storage Unlocking (DDN ExaScaler 7990X):** How replacing single-threaded desktop GIS formats with partitioned GeoParquet unlocks the full multi-gigabyte/sec parallel throughput of Waratah HPC's Lustre storage array during 3.5M+ parcel cadastral joins.
> * **Spectra Logic T950 Cold-Tier Integration:** How spatial data fingerprinting and incremental memoization prevent re-processing petabytes of static historical baselines (SLATS vegetation, 30m DEMs, multi-decade climate grids), preserving them on archival tape while streaming hot candidate parcels to compute nodes.
> * **Extreme Cost & Compute Optimization:** How decoupling heavy geometric buffering (`ST_Difference`, `ST_Buffer`) from lightweight mathematical scoring reduced cloud pipeline execution to **$0.69 USD** per full national run—a pattern that prevents HPC cluster queue exhaustion during multi-scenario policy sweeps.
> * **Zero-Cost Client Offloading:** How packaging spatial outputs for client-side **DuckDB-WASM and WebGIS** shields government HPC infrastructure from public interactive queries, delivering instant scenario modeling at zero compute cost.

---

## Executive Summary

State-level geospatial data in New South Wales is growing exponentially in volume, spatial resolution, and statutory complexity. From the 3.5M+ parcels of the NSW Digital Cadastral Data Base (DCDB) and high-resolution LiDAR elevation models, to multi-decade climate projections (NARCLiM) and environmental sensor networks under **NSW DCCEEW**, traditional single-node desktop GIS tools (`.mxd`, `.qgz`, Shapefiles) face severe scalability bottlenecks.

The **AURA Siting Crafter** project was architected on commercial cloud infrastructure (**Wherobots Cloud, AWS, and Google Cloud**) using **Apache Sedona (PySpark)**, **Apache Iceberg**, and **GeoParquet** to solve large-scale spatial siting, multi-hazard constraint analysis, and circular economy modeling.

This document synthesizes the operational, mathematical, and architectural lessons learned from AURA's cloud implementation into a roadmap for NSW Government agencies. It demonstrates how these patterns can modernize state workflows and maximize return-on-investment for specialized state computing assets such as the **NSW DCCEEW Waratah HPC Facility**.

---

## The Reference Environment: NSW DCCEEW Waratah HPC

The **Waratah High Performance Computing (HPC) facility** is a government-managed supercomputing cluster operated by the **NSW Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water (DCCEEW)** ([buy.nsw Notice C85F11C0-9984-4C2A-AE897364B13F4B7A](https://buy.nsw.gov.au/notices/C85F11C0-9984-4C2A-AE897364B13F4B7A), supported by Xenon Systems).

```
|  [ COMPUTATIONAL MANDATE ]                                     [ VENDOR & SYSTEM SUPPORT ]         |
|  Dedicated to NSW DCCEEW Policy & Science                      Maintained by Xenon Systems         |
|  • Climate Modeling (NARCLiM) & Extreme Projections            • Node reconfiguration & tuning     |
|  • Environmental Monitoring, SLATS & Biodiversity              • Continuous system maintenance     |
|  • Renewable Energy Zone (REZ) & Grid Simulations              • High-throughput interconnects     |
|  • River Basin, Flood & Water Resource Management                                                  |
|                                                                                                    |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

Unlike academic compute facilities, Waratah HPC is dedicated to direct public policy, environmental stewardship, and statutory spatial planning. AURA's cloud-proven engineering patterns offer a blueprint for running high-throughput spatial analytics across these portfolios.

---

## 5 Transferable Spatial Engineering Lessons: Cloud (AURA) to On-Premise HPC

```
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|                      AURA CLOUD PIPELINE (Wherobots / AWS / GCP)                                   |
|   15.91M Geometries | Decoupled Geometry/Scoring | GeoParquet / Iceberg | $0.69 USD Full Run       |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
                                               |
                     ARCHITECTURAL LESSONS & PATTERNS TRANSFERRED
                                               v
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
|                        NSW DCCEEW WARATAH HPC FACILITY ADOPTION                                    |
|  1. GeoParquet Parallel I/O on DDN Lustre Storage Array (ExaScaler 7990X)                          |
|  2. Memoized Partition Pruning & Cold Data Preservation (Spectra Logic T950 Tape)                  |
|  3. Decoupled Heavy Geometric Transforms from Rapid Multi-Scenario Scoring                         |
|  4. Statutory 6-Factor Multi-Hazard Scoring Engine (ARR 2019, AS 1170, AS 3959)                   |
|  5. Zero-Cost Client-Side Offloading via DuckDB-WASM for Public Planners                           |
+----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

---

### Lesson 1: Unlocking Lustre Parallel Storage via Partitioned GeoParquet

* **Cloud Experience (AURA):** Legacy shapefiles and single-file geodatabases created severe I/O bottlenecks in cloud object storage (S3/GCS). Migrating to **GeoParquet** with spatial R-Tree / Hilbert curve partitioning allowed worker nodes to read and process spatial extents independently in parallel without I/O locking.
* **HPC Translation (Waratah Lustre Array):**
  * Waratah HPC's **DDN ExaScaler 7990X Lustre filesystem** is designed for massive concurrent read/write throughput across distributed stripes.
  * Storing statewide layers (DCDB parcels, 30m DEM, biodiversity zones) in partitioned GeoParquet allows Spark/Sedona nodes to saturate Lustre striping channels simultaneously.
  * **Result:** Eliminates the I/O choke point when joining 3.5M+ NSW parcels with multi-hazard layers, completing statewide joins in minutes.

---

### Lesson 2: Decoupled Heavy Geometry vs. Lightweight Mathematical Scoring

* **Cloud Experience (AURA):** In early prototypes, adjusting a policy weight (e.g. power grid priority vs. biodiversity buffer weight) re-triggered heavy spatial joins (`ST_Buffer`, `ST_Difference`, `ST_Intersection`), driving up cloud compute costs. By decoupling geometric pre-computations (distance matrices, net developable polygons) from mathematical scoring curves (sigmoidal penalties, weighted MCDA sums), full national runs dropped to **$0.69 USD**.
* **HPC Translation (Waratah Cluster Utilization):**
  * Policy makers often need to run hundreds of "What-If" scenarios with shifting weightings (e.g., varying flood tolerance, sensitive receptor buffers, or grid distance priorities).
  * Pre-computing the geometric distance and topology layer once and caching it on Lustre allows subsequent policy scoring runs to execute as pure mathematical array operations in milliseconds.
  * **Result:** Prevents long cluster queue times and avoids wasting valuable HPC node hours on repetitive geometric recalculations.

---

### Lesson 3: Cold-Tier Data Preservation & Memoized Incremental Pipelines

* **Cloud Experience (AURA):** Large baselines (national topographic grids, geological surveys, baseline census data) rarely change between runs. Cryptographic fingerprinting (ETags, snapshot hashes) ensured untouched layers were skipped, computing only active delta updates.
* **HPC Translation (Spectra Logic T950 Tape Integration):**
  * Waratah HPC uses a **Spectra Logic T950 Tape Library** for long-term preservation of massive multi-decade climate (NARCLiM) and environmental time-series datasets.
  * Using partition fingerprinting, the Sedona spatial pipeline can pull active candidate layers to high-speed NVMe/Lustre storage while keeping cold, multi-petabyte reference datasets safely referenced in tape archives.
  * **Result:** Minimizes active primary storage consumption while guaranteeing 100% reproducible baseline provenance.

---

### Lesson 4: Standardized Multi-Hazard & Circular Physical Engineering

* **Cloud Experience (AURA):** Integrated statutory Australian standards into programmatic Python/SQL formulas rather than subjective manual GIS overlays:
  1. **Flood:** ARR 2019 / 1% AEP inundation exclusion.
  2. **Bushfire:** AS 3959:2018 BAL (Bushfire Attack Level) & PBF 2019 setbacks.
  3. **Landslide:** AGS 2007 slope susceptibility modeling ($>8.0\%$ exclusion).
  4. **Seismic:** AS 1170.4:2007 (R2018) PGA structural hazard baselines.
  5. **Wind:** AS/NZS 1170.2:2021 regional wind design speeds ($45\text{–}69\text{m/s}$).
  6. **Thermodynamic Decay & Pumped Hydro:** Spatial heat loss modeling ($T_{\text{delivery}} \le 38.5^\circ\text{C}$ within $\le 506.8\text{m}$) and void storage capacity ($E = \rho g H V \eta$).
* **HPC Translation (DCCEEW Scientific Portfolios):**
  * These validated, peer-reviewed formula implementations can be deployed as standard batch modules across DCCEEW portfolios (EnergyCo Renewable Energy Zones, DPHI Growth Areas, WaterNSW catchments).
  * **Result:** Ensures legal and statutory defensibility across state planning assessments.

---

### Lesson 5: Zero-Cost Client-Side Scenario Modeling for Planners

* **Cloud Experience (AURA):** Rather than standing up expensive, always-on spatial servers (GeoServer, ArcGIS Enterprise servers) to handle user queries, AURA exports lightweight spatial tables into client-side **DuckDB-WASM and JavaScript**. Users can dynamically drag weight sliders, apply hazard thresholds, and filter sites directly in the browser with sub-second response times.
* **HPC Translation (Protecting Government Infrastructure):**
  * Government supercomputers (like Waratah HPC) should never be directly exposed to public interactive web traffic or burdened with serving live interactive maps.
  * HPC clusters can run heavy overnight distributed batch jobs, export optimized GeoParquet artifacts, and serve interactive decision dashboards via static client-side WASM portals.
  * **Result:** Delivers sub-second interactive tools to regional council planners and executive stakeholders with zero web-tier server costs and complete cybersecurity isolation for the internal HPC cluster.

---

## Recommended Action Plan for NSW Spatial & HPC Leadership

1. **Adopt Partitioned GeoParquet as State Standard:** Transition state spatial distribution from legacy Shapefiles/FileGDBs to cloud-native GeoParquet (EPSG:7844 / EPSG:7856) to unlock parallel I/O on Lustre storage arrays.
2. **Pilot Apache Sedona on Waratah HPC:** Test distributed Apache Sedona PySpark batch scripts on the Waratah HPC cluster for state-wide Net Developable Area (NDA) generation across the 3.5M+ parcel DCDB.
3. **Establish a Decoupled Scoring Framework:** Separate heavy geometric spatial joins from multi-criteria decision scoring across DPHI, EnergyCo, and DCCEEW planning workflows to minimize compute redundancy.
4. **Integrate Output Streams with NSW Spatial Digital Twin:** Connect GeoParquet spatial outputs directly to the NSW Spatial Digital Twin and client-side DuckDB-WASM viewers for secure, zero-cost interactive policy exploration.

---

## 1. Modular PR Specifications

### Proposal 1: In-Memory DuckDB-WASM Siting Plugin (`geolibre-plugin-duckdb`)
- **Objective**: Embed DuckDB-WASM directly into GeoLibre map instances to execute high-throughput spatial joins, multi-criteria weighted scoring, and polygon area calculations locally in browser memory (<15ms latency).
- **API Surface**:
  ```typescript
  map.use(new DuckDBSpatialPlugin({
    parquetUrl: 'https://cdn.example.com/parcels.parquet',
    initialQuery: 'SELECT * FROM parcels WHERE suitability_score >= 0.7'
  }));
  ```

### Proposal 2: Area-Priority Viewport Feature Limiter (`geolibre-layer-limiter`)
- **Objective**: Prevent WebGL draw call bottlenecks when panning large cadastre layers (>50k features) by ordering by descending bounding box / polygon area and capping rendering to top $N$ features.
- **API Surface**:
  ```typescript
  layer.setRenderOptimizer({
    maxFeatures: 500,
    sortProperty: 'area_ha',
    sortOrder: 'desc'
  });
  ```

### Proposal 3: Dynamic Expression Continuous Color Ramps
- **Objective**: Provide a high-level JS helper that dynamically generates MapLibre GL interpolation expressions from arbitrary numeric data columns without rebuilding GL style sheets.

### Proposal 4: Single-File HTML Digital Twin Exporter
- **Objective**: Export the current map view, active vector micro-layers, and embedded DuckDB-WASM engine into a self-contained, air-gapped HTML file for offline stakeholder distribution.

### Proposal 5: Cryptographic Lineage & Audit Inspector
- **Objective**: Embed SHA-256 layer checksums and Apache Sedona transform recipes into the map metadata UI, providing instant provenance verification.

---

## 2. Upstream Submission Workflow
1. Fork `opengeos/GeoLibre`.
2. Create dedicated feature branches (`feature/duckdb-wasm-core`, `feature/viewport-limiter`).
3. Implement unit tests using Vitest and Playwright.
4. Submit PRs with reference benchmarks demonstrating 60fps rendering under 15M parcel loads.


## Section 4.2: Web-Native Siting Engine Specification
# LinkedIn Post: GeoLibre — Desktop Power in a Customizable Web Engine (Final Published Version)

**Target Platform:** LinkedIn  
**Audience:** Geospatial Engineers, Cloud & Data Architects, Open Source GIS Community  
**Status:** Published  

---

### Post Text

Can your browser-based GIS handle desktop-grade spatial analysis without spinning up costly cloud servers or locking you into desktop installs?

For years, GIS required a trade-off: heavyweight desktop tools (powerful, but siloed) vs. traditional WebGIS (accessible, but restricted to static tiles and sluggish server roundtrips).

Modern web-native data formats have eliminated that compromise.

Building AURA Siting Crafter with GeoLibre (opengeos/GeoLibre [Qiusheng Wu](https://www.linkedin.com/in/giswqs/)) demonstrated the unmatched agility of combining MapLibre GL, DuckDB-WASM, and cloud-native spatial data into a fully customizable web engine.

⚡ Desktop Power via Web-Native Data
Instead of moving massive files or relying on backend database compute for every slider change:
• Stream directly from cloud storage via byte-range HTTP requests (GeoParquet, PMTiles, FlatGeobuf).
• Run complex Spatial SQL in-browser using DuckDB-WASM with sub-10ms query speeds.
• Fully customize UI, layers, and analytical workflows without server infrastructure bottlenecks.

🎨 Rendering Innovations We Engineered
To maximize performance and visualization, we implemented key rendering upgrades:
1. Given a 500-feature rendering limit (for efficiency) we display the largest features first so as not to clutter the viewport. We use clustering for points.
2. Dynamic MapLibre Color Ramps: Multi-stop gradient expressions driven directly by in-memory DuckDB query attributes for instant continuous heatmaps.
3. Sub-Second Vector Streaming: Smooth 60fps pan/zoom across intricate parcel boundaries, bypassing GeoJSON payload overhead.
4. Zero-Latency Standalone Twins: Packaging layers, custom symbology, and scoring logic into single-file portable HTML apps that execute with zero network lag.

🤝 Upstream Contributions
We are planning on contributing these modular components back to OpenGeos / GeoLibre as thats why Opensource exists:
• geolibre-siting: Client-side spatial MCDA suitability scoring plugin.
• geolibre-sedona: Cloud lakehouse ETL connector (Apache Sedona / Wherobots).
• geolibre-spatial-ai: Viewport-grounded LLM Spatial SQL translation skills.
• geolibre-catalogs & geolibre-export-report: National data presets and one-click standalone HTML report packaging.

# Part V: The Spatial Siting Odyssey: Evolution & Production Milestones

# The Spatial Siting Odyssey: From Regional Brownfield Scans to Continental AI Compute & High-Precision Statutory Digital Twins

*How GetBack2Basics built AURA Siting Crafter as an open-source first commercial solution — synthesizing engineering lessons, multi-modal project inputs, an asymmetric compute architecture, deterministic AI safety sandboxes, and certified statutory due diligence services.*

**Cloud Root:** [https://aura.getback2basics.net](https://aura.getback2basics.net) • **Organization:** [GetBack2Basics](https://getback2basics.net)

---

![AURA Siting Crafter Banner](https://storage.googleapis.com/aura-siting-crafter-geolibre-app/banner.png)

## The Siting Trilemma: Energy, Water & Sovereignty

Finding optimal land for next-generation sovereign AI data centres, clean energy firming hubs, and advanced industrial ecosystems is one of the most pressing engineering bottlenecks of the 2020s.

Traditional site selection relies on coarse static GIS layers, proprietary consulting PDFs, or disjointed spreadsheets. Proponents frequently claim *"100% buildable gross site area"*, only for infrastructure developers and institutional funds to discover years later that 40% of the parcel is locked by uninsurable 1% AEP floodways, mine subsidence strain zones, or EPA acoustic trigger buffers.

To de-risk capital allocations, **GetBack2Basics** engineered **AURA Siting Crafter** through a five-stage evolutionary journey. Built as an **open-source first commercial platform**, AURA combines a transparent, peer-reviewed open geospatial engine with certified statutory-grade site assessment services.

Here is the full synthesis of how we built it, the hard engineering lessons learned, the asymmetric cloud economics, the deterministic AI safety sandbox, and how developers can leverage custom project-specific reports.

---

```mermaid
graph TD
    S1["1. Basic Regional GIS<br/>(Hunter Brownfield Exploration)"] --> S2["2. High-Data Micro-Siting<br/>(1m LiDAR, Cadastre & Cooling Loops)"]
    S2 --> S3["3. Continental Scale-Out<br/>(17 National Candidate Hubs)"]
    S3 --> S4["4. National Multi-Hazard MCDA<br/>(ARR 2019, Seismic PGA & TCHA Wind)"]
    S4 --> S5["5. Statutory Forensic Digital Twins<br/>(Automated Multi-Project Pipeline)"]
    
    style S1 fill:#1e293b,stroke:#3b82f6,color:#f8fafc
    style S2 fill:#1e293b,stroke:#06b6d4,color:#f8fafc
```
